Photo / Philip Fong , AFP <p> The UK , Japan and South Korea sweltered this year through the hottest summers since each country began keeping records , their weather agencies said . <p> Temperatures the world over have soared in recent years as human-induced climate change creates ever more erratic weather patterns . <p> The UK 's provisional mean June-August temperature was 16.1C , which was 1.51C above the long-term average and surpassed all years since 1884 , including the previous record , set in 2018 , the Met Office said . <p> The British summer had four heatwaves , below-average rainfall and sustained sunshine , and followed the nation 's warmest spring in more than a century . <p> Japan 's average temperature spike was even starker over the same three summer months , at 2.36C above " the standard value " , making it the hottest since records began @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said . <p> Tourists and Seoul residents use UV-blocking umbrellas and hats to shield themselves from the scorching sun as they walk past the statue of King Sejong in Gwanghwamun Square in Seoul , South Korea . Photo / Getty Images <h> National disaster <p> South Korea is meanwhile grappling with a prolonged drought that has hit the eastern coastal city of Gangneung . <p> A state of national disaster has been declared in the city of 200,000 people , with water levels at the Obong reservoir , the city 's main source of piped water , falling below 15% . <p> The dry spell has forced authorities to implement water restrictions , including shutting off 75% of household meters . <p> The UK 's provisional record this year means all of its five warmest summers have taken place this century . <p> The Met Office noted " a summer as hot or hotter than 2025 is now 70 times more likely than it would be in a ' natural ' climate with no human-caused greenhouse gas emissions " . <p> But the speed of temperature increases across the world @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has warmed the fastest per decade since 1990 , followed closely by Asia , according to global data from the US National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration . <p> The United Nations warned last month that rising global temperatures are having an ever-worsening impact on the health of workers , and also hitting productivity , which they say dropped 2-3% for every degree above 20C.

@@128870695 <p> S&amp;P Global Ratings says New Zealand needs to make progress balancing its revenue and spending before borrowing more money , even though public debt remains relatively low . <p> The credit rating agency held a panel discussion in Wellington on Wednesday where its analysts discussed a wide range of issues with the Institute of Financial Professionals NZ ( INFINZ ) . <p> Martin Foo , a director and lead analyst , said when overseas investors look at the Crown 's finances they think : " Great balance sheet , but crap profit and loss " . <p> He said public debt was relatively low by global standards , particularly compared to other developed countries , but it had increased more than average during the pandemic . <p> " I would say that debt is not a concern , per se , but it 's the cost of servicing debt that is more of an issue . We are in a situation now where New Zealand spends a lot more on interest expenses than it does on other major items , such as defence . " <p> Foo said he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ had called on the Government to further leverage its strong balance to support the economic recovery . <p> " We would say ... although the absolute level of debt is low , the ramp up in debt has been substantial . Ideally , you 'd want to have some fiscal buffers for the next crisis . The stock of debt is not so much a concern as is the cost of that debt , " he said . <p> New Zealand 's mismatch between revenue and spending means having to service higher debt levels would reduce the Crown 's ability to continue other kinds of day-to-day spending . <p> " The Government has given a lot of the right signals around trying to crowd in private sector investments , do more public-private partnerships , and novel transaction structures . But so far we 've yet to see the evidence of those working at scale , " Foo said . <p> The analyst said New Zealand had a stable outlook on its AA+ foreign currency credit rating , the second highest possible , which means it was unlikely to be downgraded @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ domestic currency rating is AAA , the highest possible rating , with a stable outlook . Going by the view count this article seems to have been well received , so let 's do some more unpacking of Stanford 's love fest with the knowledge curriculum . <p> Once again I am going to base this article on Bevan Holloway 's very thorough and comprehensive delving into the hidden underworld of Stanford 's education agenda . <p> I will highlight specific sections and add my own commentary . <p> This particular article is wide ranging and there 's so much of value that I really encourage you all to read it . <p> The opening paragraph is about Elizabeth Rata -- much has already been written about her pernicious influence on Stanford , so I do n't need to revisit it here . <p> However I 'm sure you will be pleased to know that Erica Stanford is an educational expert based on a life time of study and research .... yeah , right . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ had a profound impact on her : E D Hirsch 's The Schools We Need and Why We Do n't Have Them . Hirsch founded the Core Knowledge Foundation in the USA . " <p> So there you are , that 's the foundation for her education agenda . Most ( all ? ) of her policies derive from here , influenced by the Ministerial Advisory Group ( MAG ) led by cognitive psychologist Dr Michael Johnston -- we will come back to the role of cognitive psychology later on . Anyone care to guess who may have recommended Hirsch 's book to Stanford ? <p> She obviously became a convert to the cause , overlooking the fact that Hirsch and others promoting the core knowledge curricula are but one very small part of the extensive number of pedagogical researchers who go back at least to John Dewey in the early years of the 20th century , a scholar whose educational influence was felt in New Zealand education until 1990 . Many other luminaries have also contributed to this research , e.g. , Dr Guy Claxon , whom I will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ these people or she has chosen to ignore them . Take your pick . <p> " In June , she went to their conference and shared a stage with Hirsch , along with Sir Nicholas Gibb , former UK schools minister and Paul Givan , Northern Ireland 's Minister of Education . The plenary session was led by AEI Senior Fellow Robert Pondiscio . <p> Now , the AEI is a right-wing think-tank with connections to the Koch brothers . They were leading champions of the neoliberal project during George W. Bush 's presidency , and remain a significant force in right-wing American politics . They have a history of distorting the science of climate change , and lead campaigns for school choice , among other things . " <p> The very fact she has chosen to associate herself with this group is a very clear indication of the ideology she is bringing to her role as Minister of Education , and , by extension , also signals the ideology and influences of the government as a whole . To repeat myself from previous articles , she is known by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the conference . I 'm wondering what qualified her to be a speaker here ? Her presentation starts at 15.23 , should you want to watch it and if your stomach is strong enough ... <p> " Stanford spoke fluently about the curriculum reform process she has led -- " based on your book " she said to Hirsch -- and hit all the key words as she espoused the virtue of the changes : knowledge-rich , science of learning , automatising , cognitive load , serve and return ... you get the idea . " <p> And then : <p> " She also spoke about how all brains learn the same , regardless of circumstance or culture , and therefore all kids deserve this approach as it 's how we 'll address the equity gap . <p> In that one sentence she reveals she does n't know what she is talking about . What about ADHD or autistic kids , for a start ? <p> She continued : <p> " no matter what culture ... what family you 're from you have the very best possible instruction , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on the science of learning , that even if you 're from a poor family and you do n't have the resources at home , when you walk into that classroom you have everything you need to succeed the same as every other child -- that 's the reforms we 're driving . " <p> Hmmm ... <p> And lastly : <p> " sometimes you can misdiagnose the problem and think it 's a ... we need to be more cultural , that will help with our reading and writing . Well , it 'll be good because it 's important to help children feel comfortable in the classroom , but reading , writing , and maths is around the curriculum and explicit teaching in the science of learning " . <p> So here we are back in the days of the Key led governments when all that was needed to address education achievement was proper teaching and standardised assessments , totally ignoring the wealth of worldwide research that shows the biggest impact on children 's learning is inequality and deprivation , not just in physical needs such as food @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of out of school experiences . <p> The phrase ' Science of Learning ' is frequently referenced by Stanford and those influencing her so it 's time to unpick this . <p> This science comes from the cognitive psychology world ( which brings us to Dr Michael Johnston ) , and studies how people acquire , process , and retain knowledge and list a number of key principles . If you want to know more ask the AI of your choice . <p> Effective learning leverages these principles to optimise attention , memory , and application of knowledge ( so far so good ) but this is where the issues start . <p> The Knowledge Curriculum devotees envisage classrooms where children are fed knowledge in a teacher led ' didactic ' process , akin to university lectures for those who 've experienced them . <p> This is where they divert away from the century 's worth of pedagogy that has researched how children actually learn ( research Jean Piaget if you 'd like an example ) . <p> A few articles back I referenced Brazilian educator Paolo Friere 's banking @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the Oppressed ' ) <p> " Education is thus seen as a process of depositing knowledge into passive students . Teachers are the epistemological authority in this system ; students ' pre-existing knowledge is ignored , aside from what was expected to be ' deposited ' into them earlier . Freire also refers to a banking paradigm as regarding students to be " adaptable , manageable beings . ... The more completely they accept the passive role imposed on them , the more they tend simply to adapt to the world as it is and to the fragmented view of reality deposited in them . " ( Wikipedia ) <p> " There are no good scientific grounds for believing that only Direct Instruction ' works ' and that anything that involves students in thinking , grappling and discussing before they have everything explained to them ' does n't work ' . <p> Such sweeping and simplistic claims are not supported by an accurate and up-to-date reading of the research literature . What the research shows is that didactic teaching is good for some audiences , for some topics , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> He explains that direct instruction does have its place , e.g. , in the teaching and learning of skills and rote knowledge -- learning times tables for example , or for short term gains in test performance : <p> But : <p> " ... if your aim is for deeper and more long-term understanding , or for the parallel cultivation of useful , transferable dispositions such as self-discipline , intellectual humility , curiosity , skepticism and imagination , then ' direct instruction ' is not the pedagogy of choice . " <p> The next quotes are key to the discussion over Stanford 's embrace of the Knowledge Curriculum : <p> " My fight is only with a particularly virulent form of traditionalist extremism that claims ( a ) that there is only one effective method of teaching , ( b ) that this one size fits all students , all topics and all purposes ; and , most critically , ( c ) that cognitive science provides an unarguable mandate for such a view . " <p> And : <p> " In particular these pronouncements have been gleefully @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ antiquated and simplistic model of education . " <p> To pre-empt the inevitable comments about New Zealand education being in crisis , this is unfounded and any current issues with achievement are the results of a ) inequality and b ) the last National led government 's attempt to standardise education through National Standards . <p> The frequent claims about this ' crisis ' are made by those who have their own agenda , for example the New Zealand Initiative , to justify the imposition of their own ideologies . <p> So there you have it . This is the untested education system Stanford wants to impose on the country 's children , an ideology that disregards the wealth of pedagogical knowledge . <p> The tragedy is that our children are the guinea pigs for this , and it is their future that is at risk . <h> 2 COMMENTS <p> Ask any parent or real teacher and they will tell you that all kids do not learn the same . The parents ' ability is a major factor in the child 's ability , along with any substance use during pregnancy , the family 's financial circumstances , and the quality of teaching ; all these factors contribute to what children achieve in school . I can agree that it is unfair to label children as failures if they do n't pass certain exams , although it is equally clear that not all children can become brain surgeons , for instance . Our society has the habit of glorifying winners and ignoring the also-rans , which may be acceptable in sports . Still , when applied in education , it creates an unnecessary barrier in the future choices of those who do not succeed academically . We need high standards in various occupations , so I believe we should offer suitable career choices for individuals with different abilities , enabling them to contribute to and enrich society through their efforts .

@@128888835 <h> Patients first or politics first ? There 's a much bigger picture <p> Health reforms and funding changes are only part of the story ; wider social and economic factors remain critical to Kiwis ' wellbeing Image : Anna Jackowska on Unsplash <p> Recent wins for general practice come with caveats , while a new report reminds us healthcare makes up only 20 per cent of what keeps us healthy . The real drivers are social , economic and environmental and tackling them takes more than slogans , writes Barbara Fountain <p> Capitation reweighting , but no ethnicity factor . <p> Registrar training funding , but it must come from Health New Zealand Te Whatu Ora baseline funding . <p> New capitation funding with less impact on GP bank accounts than expected . <p> Urgent-care money with a puzzling distribution pattern . <p> New online services , but no outcomes data . <p> There has been a lot of good news for general practice recently , but there are often fishhooks or qualifying details yet to be determined . <p> On the upside , the sector can be pleased @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ radar . On the downside , a strong sense of political necessity remains -- that the catchphrase " patients first " is as much about securing votes as it is about creating a future-proof health service . <p> Nevertheless , as troubling as the problems are , a report out last month delivers something precious to the health service and politicians alike ; a reminder that no matter how we angst over changes , for political ends or otherwise , healthcare contributes only about 20 per cent to what it means to be healthy even on a really good day . <p> Yes , that gnarly multi-tentacled beastie , the social determinants of health , made an appearance , spread over 200 pages of facts , figures , graphs and the occasional totara tree in the report , Determining our Future , from the Public Health Advisory Committee , an independant expert advisory committee created under the Pae Ora legislation . <p> The report comes just over 25 years after a similar one from the once-was National Health Committee . The nub is that , despite some overall progress since @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , disability and age have persisted across determinants of health , including income , employment and education . <p> Indicators have declined for housing affordability , household crowding , food security , and the quality of our natural environment . And levels of trust in the Government and within communities have also decreased . <p> The report contains a wealth of stats . Here are a few to mull over . <p> Children growing up in our most urban areas can expect to live four years longer than those in the most rural areas . <p> A Pakeha baby boy born in Waikato can expect to live eight years longer than his Maori neighbour . <p> Pakeha children can expect to live to 84 in the northern region , seven years longer than Pacific peoples ' children -- a gap that has increased since 2000 . <p> Since 2000 , the life-expectancy gap between Maori and other ethnicities has been narrowing . It was 8.3 years in 2001/03 and had reduced to 6.6 years by 2020/22 , a 20 per cent decrease . Inequities in some other health outcomes have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ But we are less physically active and more of us have unhealthy excess weight , contributing to diabetes , cancers and disability . <p> We learn that almost two in five people find it hard to meet everyday costs ; two-thirds of New Zealanders do not feel " well off " , and one in four sometimes do not have enough to eat . <p> The distribution of deprivation is highly unequal . More than half the Maori population lives in the most deprived neighbourhoods . The Pacific population is even more skewed towards the most deprived deciles . <p> The report finds the distribution of wealth is similarly unequal . Surveys from 2015 to 2021 show the wealthiest 10 per cent of households hold about 50 per cent of the country 's household financial assets . The poorest 20 per cent of households hold only 1 per cent of the country 's household net wealth . <p> Research by the Treasury and Inland Revenue shows that our current taxation system results in the wealthiest 300 families in New Zealand paying an effective tax rate that is less than half @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ years ago , when the first report was written , " climate change " rated a single mention in the references , while artificial intelligence was a science fiction dream . <p> Now these two factors dominate as the report looks forward to 2040 . Climate change brings challenges to food supply , disease spread and housing . There 's even a passing mention of glacial melt . <p> AI and the digital environment offer potential for good , but also the potential to increase harm from discrimination , disinformation , and the exacerbation of inequities , not least through jobs lost . In the next decade , the report suggests , at least 30 per cent of the tasks of managers , professionals , technicians , machinery operators and drivers , 40 per cent of the functions of sales workers , and 50 per cent of the tasks of clerical and administrative workers will be effectively done by AI . <p> If the current Coalition Government ca n't take on board health equity because it is the right thing to do , then it should do so for its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this report , the message is clear : social and economic prosperity are linked and depend on each other . And in that prosperity is partnership with Maori , and , despite current government policy , this report finds increasing recognition of the promise and potential that Te Tiriti o Waitangi offers . <p> I am pleased Mr Brown has primary care on his radar . And whether " putting patients first " goes beyond a political slogan , time will tell . However , as the report shows , improving services will not be enough to enhance the health of people and the economy . Somewhere in those 200 pages , I read : " People do n't live their lives in silos . " <p> Mr Brown needs a wider brief : " Putting people first . @@128912689 <p> Explainer - When asked this week about ACT 's call to withdraw from the Paris Agreement on climate change , Prime Minister Christopher Luxon was quick to reject the idea . <p> Pulling out of Paris , the global accord to keep global warming below 2 degrees Celsius , would be " the quickest way " to hurt New Zealand farmers , he said . <p> " Our competitor countries would like nothing more than to see New Zealand products off their shelves . And I 'm telling you , having worked in large multinationals , they would just move to another supplier anyway . <p> Luxon underlined that farming has been kept out of the Emissions Trading Scheme - a long-standing exemption - and said : " Very , very shortly , we 'll have more to say about methane targets . " <p> Those comments set the stage for the government 's controversial proposal to adopt a new principle for methane : " no additional warming . " <p> Lost ? Here 's what it means : <h> The great methane debate explained <p> New Zealand 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's cows . Or more specifically , the methane they emit - a greenhouse gas far more potent than carbon dioxide in the short term , and one that makes up almost half of the country 's emissions . <p> Currently , the easiest way to cut methane is to cut the number of cows ( and sheep and deer , who also produce the gas ) . But because cutting the number of cows could lead to lower milk production , and milk production is considered vital to farmers , business and the economy , managing methane emissions in this way is deeply unpopular among the farming community . <p> They 're also deeply opposed to paying for their emissions in the same way other industries do , under the Emissions Trading Scheme , because it would impact profits . <p> That 's where " no additional warming " comes in . <h> What is " no additional warming " ? <p> Methane behaves differently to carbon dioxide . CO2 stays in the atmosphere for centuries , meaning even the strongest emissions reduction efforts can only stabilise its heating @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ breaks down in around a decade . That means if emissions stay flat , the amount of methane in the air stops growing . In theory , that prevents further warming from methane . <p> Methane makes up almost half of the country 's emissions . Photo : Adam Simpson <p> This is the idea behind " no additional warming " . Instead of driving methane towards net zero , like with CO2 , the proposal is to cut it just enough to hold its heating effect steady at a fixed point - in this case , 2017 levels , when New Zealand wrote its climate change targets into law . <h> The alternative <p> The Climate Change Commission ( CCC ) , the independent body tasked with advising the government on emissions , says this is n't enough . <p> Methane is around 80 times more powerful than CO2 over 20 years . It does n't linger as long , but while it 's in the air it drives a huge share of warming . In New Zealand , it accounts for nearly two-thirds of the temperature rise the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a major cause of climate change and one of the industries worst-affected by the havoc it wreaks . Photo : Samantha Gee <p> Because of this , the CCC instead recommends deeper methane reductions of 35-47 percent by 2050 . That would n't just stop methane making things worse , but actually cool the planet in the near term , buying time while the harder task of decarbonising CO2 plays out over decades . <h> Why farmers back " no additional warming " <p> Farming groups such as Federated Farmers and Beef + Lamb see " no additional warming " as a fairer goal . They argue that asking farmers to cut beyond stabilisation is like asking them to remove carbon dioxide from the atmosphere - a demand never placed on fossil fuel companies or industries that emit CO2 . <p> The government has echoed these concerns , warning that pushing farmers too hard could drive production overseas to places with dirtier practices , a phenomenon known as " emissions leakage . " <h> Why critics call it an " accounting trick " <p> Lawyers for Climate Action call it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of sidestepping the CCC 's statutory role and watering down climate ambition . <p> International scientists have been even more blunt , accusing New Zealand ( and Ireland , which has floated similar ideas ) of an " accounting trick " that allows high-emitting agricultural nations to lock in their current level of pollution as acceptable . <p> The result , they argue , is that the atmosphere stays hotter than it needs to be , while poorer countries and other sectors bear the burden of cutting harder . <h> The wider context <p> Ever since New Zealand signed the Kyoto Protocol in the 1990s , governments have floated ways to make agriculture pay for its emissions . <p> Farmers have successfully fought back , keeping agriculture out of the Emissions Trading Scheme and getting rid of a Labour-led scheme to introduce farm levies . <p> The " no additional warming " approach can be seen as the latest step in that campaign : by lowering the scale of required cuts , it would help protect farmers from higher emissions costs or herd reductions that would hit production . <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ embrace the " no additional warming " principle or hold the line on the current , more ambitious cuts . <p> It faces pressure from farmers to lower the target , and from climate advocates - and its own independent climate commission - not to .

Epstein . Epstein , however , has a fraught reputation . @@128936954 <h> David Seymour on leaving the Paris Agreement , Koroneihana &amp; the Tamaki Makaurau by-election <p> This week ACT leader and Deputy Prime Minister David Seymour reaffirmed his party 's call for New Zealand to exit the Paris Agreement unless " realistic " emissions targets can be negotiated . <p> The Paris Agreement is the world 's most significant international treaty on climate change , aiming to limit global warming and reduce greenhouse gas emissions . Signed in 2015 at the United Nations Climate Change Conference ( COP21 ) in Paris , the agreement brought together nearly 200 countries ; including Aotearoa New Zealand ; in a shared commitment to address the climate crisis . <p> At its heart , the Paris Agreement seeks to keep the rise in global average temperatures " well below " 2 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels , while pursuing efforts to limit warming to 1.5 degrees . Scientists say this threshold is critical to avoiding the worst impacts of climate change , including more extreme weather events , sea-level rise , and loss of biodiversity . <p> Each country is required to set its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( NDCs ) , and update them every five years . The agreement also commits wealthy countries to supporting developing nations with climate finance , technology , and capacity-building to adapt to climate impacts . <p> Unlike earlier treaties , the Paris Agreement is built on flexibility . Countries set their own goals , but must report regularly on their progress and face international scrutiny . This transparency is designed to create accountability and encourage nations to raise their ambition over time . <p> New Zealand , for example , has pledged to cut net greenhouse gas emissions by 50 percent below 2005 levels by 2030 . Achieving that goal will require major changes in transport , energy , and agriculture ; sectors where emissions remain high . <p> While widely celebrated as a breakthrough , the Paris Agreement has faced criticism . Some argue it does not go far enough , since countries ' targets are voluntary and collectively fall short of the 1.5-degree goal . Others point to delays in delivering promised climate finance to poorer nations , many of which are already experiencing severe impacts . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ signed , although progress in renewable energy , electric vehicles , and climate policy has accelerated in recent years . <p> As countries prepare for the next round of UN climate talks , pressure is growing on governments to move faster and more boldly . The Paris Agreement remains the cornerstone of international climate cooperation -- but whether it succeeds will depend on how seriously nations act on their promises . <h> Follow us <h> Stay Updated <p> Radio Waatea is Auckland 's only Maori radio station that provides an extensive bi-lingual broadcast to its listeners . Based at Nga Whare Waatea marae in Mangere , it is located in the middle of the biggest Maori population in Aotearoa.

" The most remarkable thing about the first four months of Reform being in power is how unremarkable it is , " said Tony Travers , a professor of politics at the London School of Economics . <p> In Britain , councils like Warwickshire run services from repairing roads to social work . <p> While Finch is the political leader , he works alongside an unelected , non-partisan chief executive , Monica Fogarty , and her team of civil servants , who oversee the day-to-day running of services . <p> In the run-up to the local elections , Reform announced a Doge unit modelled on the US initiative that Elon Musk labelled the Department of Government Efficiency . And after years of austerity across Britain , municipal spending contains little fat to cut . <p> Finch , in a navy suit and pocket handkerchief , makes a point of differentiating himself from the average teenager and mocks those his age who dress more casually . <p> They are , he said , " tracksuit gangster wannabe kids " , who " need to take a chill pill and really realise where they are in the world " . <p> The area Finch leads encompasses Warwick , with its medieval castle and 700-year-old timbered buildings , as well as more gritty towns like Bedworth and Nuneaton . <p> His mother was a hairdresser and factory worker in Nuneaton who is now a teaching assistant ; his father used to work in construction . <p> He briefly flirted with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ MPs in 2022 . Many councils are also responsible for sensitive policy areas including care for vulnerable children . <p> Slashing spending on projects to combat climate change is another target for Reform , which has received donations from investors in fossil fuels . @@128953700 <h> Sean Plunket now stands alone on his Platform <p> Exclusive : After being cut off by founding funders the Wright family , Sean Plunket has taken full control of his anti-woke media brand The Platform . Duncan Greive analyses the changes . <p> Wayne Wright Jr , previously the majority shareholder of the digital audio brand The Platform , has sold his entire stake in the business to its founder and breakfast host , Sean Plunket . The company was launched in early 2022 , a year after Plunket was removed from the talkback station MagicTalk , following a successful BSA complaint that an interview " had the effect of reflecting and amplifying casual racism towards Maori " . Not long after came an advertiser revolt , and Plunket left the station under strained circumstances . <p> Plunket licked his wounds , located a backer in Wright , and created a media brand devoted to being able to say almost anything and talk to almost anyone . Now he has total control over the venture , as well as total responsibility for its financial viability . <p> The transaction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Platform has come under mounting financial pressure . It parted company with popular sports talk host Martin Devlin a year ago , after the Wright family started winding down its funding , and cut it off completely in March of 2025 . Devlin had earlier attracted a major corporate sponsor in One NZ -- ironically one of the same companies ( when known as Vodafone ) which had previously left Magic Talk in protest at its content . <p> This reveals a meaningful change in the commercial environment for The Platform . " Those first few months we could n't give away our advertising , " Wright recalled to Plunket on the station 's third birthday earlier this year . " Everyone was afraid of so-called cancel culture . " Not any more . Along with One NZ , The Platform 's YouTube clips have Spark ads running as mid-rolls . It shows the more permissive media environment we operate in now , a move away from a period when blue chip advertisers attempted to distance themselves from content considered objectionable . <p> The station is part of a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ around characters coming out the other side of mainstream broadcasting , including a revived Sports Cafe , the Dom Harvey Show and Reality Check Radio . All have created significant new enterprises from scratch , despite employing some hosts who exited mainstream organisations under various shapes of cloud . <h> A new , unmediated media <p> Their success shows that mainstream and regulated media has to obey a set of rules around content which are quite different to those which governed radio in previous eras -- and different again from the more libertarian era of social media we live through now . MagicTalk was overseen by the BSA ; The Platform answers to no regulator . The combination of wide social distribution through TikTok and Instagram and massive platforms like YouTube and Spotify has proven the audience for shock jocks and unbridled talkback never went away -- it just moved . <p> Video , which generates huge engagement and wide distribution , is a major part of this movement -- both short clips on social media and longer forms on streaming platforms . These new startups can generate engagement that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Platform exists within this loose group , but also connects more directly to mainstream politics and culture . It has hosted Chrises Luxon and Hipkins , former All Black coaches Ian Foster and Steve Hansen , along with internationally infamous figures like Candace Owens and Posie Parker . It has persistently positioned itself as the only honest and uncompromised media outlet in New Zealand , and invites a wide variety of guests , though gives them quite different hearings . <p> It 's a persistent troll -- weather is called " climate change " , brought to you by Kiwi Fertiliser -- but not completely incorrigible , as they pronounce te reo place names correctly within . Plunket is still brilliant at times . Just this morning he opened with an arch critique of Luxon 's announcement of Ikea 's opening day , which he framed as a distraction from the Amazon data centre debacle . " That 's not investing in New Zealand , " he said of Ikea 's debut , " it 's opening a shop . " His interview with Ray Chung was a masterclass @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Sean Plunket during his interview with Ray Chung . Photo : The Platform . <p> The Platform has also , inevitably and intentionally , attracted its share of controversy , much of it of Plunket 's own making and fairly despicable . In 2023 he baselessly claimed that Stuff was underwritten by the Ngai Tahu iwi . In 2024 he referred to endangered Maui dolphins as " the Down syndrome kids of marine mammals . They 're cross-bred and they deserve to die . " He called for a " pogrom " of appointees from the previous government , in an interview with David Seymour . It 's not just on air . Last year he acknowledged " appalling " behaviour in an employment tribunal hearing involving former Platform staffer Ani O'Brien . Then there was that tweet about Gaza , about which more later . <p> If Plunket has a favourite drum to bang , it 's critiquing his former employers . He was a mainstream media lifer , and some consider him among the best hosts Morning Report ever had -- but his view has markedly soured in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of his beat . In one of the Platform 's most-viewed clips , he talks about " watching the bribe in action " on TVNZ 's Breakfast , while another popular clip is entitled " Sean Plunket explains to Stuff how journalism works " . Just this morning , he was meant to interview Stacey Morrison , at her request , as part of a forthcoming Te Mangai Paho-funded show for TVNZ , entitled I 'm Not Racist , But ... <p> He had been hyping it , and was furious when it was cancelled less than 24 hours out , with the production citing it having not gained the necessary permission from TVNZ . The state broadcaster told The Spinoff in a statement that " we 're keen for Stacey to promote the show , we just want her to do that when she can tell people how and when they can watch . Our view is that any interviews have been delayed rather than cancelled . We 'll support the media she wants to engage in when it can help viewership . " <p> Plunket 's big opportunity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ inflammatory subject ( " I do n't know why they 'd want to talk to us about that " , he said , implausibly ) had been dashed , but it was all just more content . He ridiculed TVNZ to open his show this morning , and decided to pointedly replace Morrison with a return visit for Julian Batchelor , the Christian evangelist who leads the Stop Co-governance movement . <h> A new era , with a new kind of media backer <p> Wright Jr funded The Platform due to his dissatisfaction with mainstream broadcasting in the aftermath of the pandemic , and its paranoid thesis is made clear in the statement establishing a change of ownership . " Established during dark times in New Zealand journalism where the State exchanged control of public narrative for cash and public confidence in media integrity was at all-time lows , the Wright Family funded The Platform from initial startup to a self-sustaining media company to confirm the viability of an unbiased non taxpayer funded new media enterprise in New Zealand . " <p> This has not been uncomplicated for the Wright @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Spinoff that " they ca n't cancel me . When people come and rattle their sabres at us , we can say , ' well , tomorrow is another day . Good luck with that . And by the way , you could buy one of our things over here . Stay in a hotel we own ' . " Yet that has not been entirely true . <p> As Webworm reported , Plunket recently wrote on X , during a period when famine in Palestine was widely covered , " I 'm really concerned about the mass outbreak of anorexia in Gaza " . Wright says that " folks turned to the Wright family for comment . But the Wright family are no longer owners of the Platform . " <p> Wayne Wright Jr ( Photo : Supplied ; Design : Tina Tiller ) <p> That was because Wright had bought out the family foundation 's interests in March , transferring them to NGEN Trustee Ltd , a vehicle controlled solely by Wright Jr . The terms of the sale have not been disclosed , but given the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ made a substantial financial loss on their investment in the Platform . <p> Profit was hardly the point , and there seem to be no hard feelings . Wright told The Spinoff that " Sean and I have been very close over the past four years . We started this thing from scratch . I 've shared a lot of my personal life with him , he 's shared a lot with me . I 'd like to think that over the past four years we 've become friends as well as business partners . " Plunket echoes this sentiment . <p> This transaction is the end of their business affair . It allows Plunket to assume total control of the enterprise he started , and has made in his image : mischievous , sometimes obnoxious , and always keen to pick a fight . <p> This morning 's Morrison story will pass . Will The Platform ? It was financed into being by Wright , but now rests solely on Plunket 's shoulders . Neither Plunket nor Wright would disclose the terms , though Plunket did say he found @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and is adamant that there is no new backer . He readily admits that he was not a businessperson prior to starting the station , and that recent months have seen it dipping in and out of breakeven . It relies on a mix of audience revenue , revenue share from YouTube and commercial partnerships to pay its expenses . <p> While before it could call on one of New Zealand 's most vast family fortunes for backup , now it 's a much more precarious proposition . Ultimately , from this day forward , The Platform is Plunket 's alone . <h> Subscribe <h> All Events <h> FOLLOW US <h> DOWNLOAD THE APP <p> The Spinoff is subject to NZ Media Council procedures . @@129020545 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> The configuration of our coalition government requires consensus between all three parties to advance a new policy -- and all three have economic philosophies that are often irreconcilable . Photos / Getty Images <p> The configuration of our coalition government requires consensus between all three parties to advance a new policy -- and all three have economic philosophies that are often irreconcilable . Photos / Getty Images <p> It 's worth reminding ourselves why advanced economies use independent central banks to conduct their monetary policy , given the controversial role the Reserve Bank of New Zealand has played in our political and economic lives during the past five years -- and the mess around the departure of governor Adrian Orrin March and more recently board chair Neil Quigley . <p> These banks have the power to inflate away the value of our incomes and life savings , then bring inflation down again by plunging the economy into a deep recession , costing people their jobs , businesses , homes . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ control . The government can not direct the bank , although we recently learnt the prime minister regularly gives his reckons to the monetary policy committee , who are surely dazzled by his observations that interest rates should be low and credit should be cheap . <p> In New Zealand there 's a single-word explanation for this undemocratic model : Muldoon . He pressured the bank to lower interest rates in election years , making mortgages and loans cheaper , speeding up the economy , pushing up wages . The consequence was high and sustained inflation -- it hit 18.4% in 1980 . In comparison , the post-Covid price surge never went above 7.3% . Muldoon is the poster child for independent central banking , proving that regulating the money supply is too important to be left to politicians . We empower the technocrats on the condition they remain scrupulously apolitical and publicly accountable for their actions . <p> National and Act did not feel governor Orr was apolitical . They took umbrage at his promotion of climate change and progressive interpretation of the principles of the Treaty of Waitangi @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ were contentious issues . <p> There are still grave reservations about the RBNZ 's judgment during and after Covid , and Orr did not welcome scrutiny of his enormously consequential decisions , bristling with indignation when challenged by journalists and opposition MPs . After he stood down from his role without any explanation , the bank 's chair declared Orr resigned for " personal reasons " . No one believed this . <p> After a six-month media battle to drag the truth out of the nation 's most powerful financial institution , it emerged Orr had clashed with Finance Minister Nicola Willis and Treasury officials about the bank 's budget . He 'd stepped down a week before his departure was made public . <p> It also became apparent Reserve Bank chair Neil Quigley had fought a vigorous campaign to prevent any of the background being made public . Willis was aware of all of this but chose not to disclose it , and kept Quigley on as chair until it emerged in the media . Her office claimed Quigley had chosen to hand over to a new chair " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " -- a second misleading press release to address the consequences of the first . <h> Critique catches on <p> There 's a critique of independent central banking that has circulated on the left for many years and is gaining credibility among the new right as Donald Trump takes on the independence of the US Federal Reserve . <p> This argues that central banks symbolise everything that 's wrong with modern political economies . Decisions that should be made by elected politicians have been placed beyond democratic accountability . Instead they 're made by economists who are allegedly impartial but have their own ideologies and agendas they advance beyond a veneer of neutrality . They work in concert with other central bankers around the world against the interests of their own citizens , prioritising price stability and currency value over more egalitarian goals like full employment or higher wages for workers . Elections and political debates merely deliver the illusion of democracy . <p> This critique is probably wrong : we 'd be better off with Orr on a bad day than if Chris Hipkins or Christopher Luxon could game @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ culture of imperious secrecy around the bank is not encouraging . People will believe the bank is malevolent if its leaders behave like villains . And the government 's near total powerlessness in the economic sphere is striking . It is desperate to be seen to be doing things without doing anything . <h> Let 's go shopping <p> Shortly before Quigley 's resignation Willis announced her plans for the grocery sector . After five months of investigation she was creating a fast-track regime for building new supermarkets -- a policy that could have been announced five months ago ( and should have been introduced 10 years ago . ) Even if a new entrant enters the market and builds these supermarkets it could take years -- decades -- for food prices to equilibrate . <p> A few days after that , Luxon revealed an exciting new plan to build an Amazon data centre in Auckland , investing billions of dollars , creating over 1000 jobs . Labour pointed out it made the same announcement in 2021 , and it 's not clear if Amazon is going to build anything @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> That same day , Smiths City -- which has operated for more than 100 years -- went into voluntary administration . <p> It would all be comic if it was n't so bleak . <p> But it 's not a cabal of economists that 's keeping the government from acting . Instead , it 's a combination of conservatism , vested interests and the configuration of the coalition government , which requires consensus between all three parties to advance a new policy -- and all three have economic philosophies that are often irreconcilable . <p> This is n't a problem that can be solved by replacing the prime minister or finance minister , and it will get worse if the parties of the right somehow scratch and claw their way to re-election next year as National 's conspicuous weakness strengthens Act and New Zealand First . 
@@129020560 <h> The five most bizarro moments from the last day of the NZ First convention <p> In Palmerston North , Winston Peters , NZ First and their band of faithfuls never lost sight of the fight : the culture wars . <p> In the span of seven hours , the word " woke " was said 21 times in speeches during the last day of the New Zealand First convention in Palmerston North . There were robust debates , policy announcements , the least surprising " surprise " speaker in former Labour minister Stuart Nash and a few too many security guards for the number of protestors there were holding greyhounds . <p> The big policy announcement was Peters ' early campaign promise that NZ First would raise compulsory employee and employer Kiwisaver contributions to 10% . It would be funded by cutting taxes elsewhere . <p> But mostly it was a hectic affair and The Spinoff braved the mean streets of the Manawatu to deliver the moments worth remembering ( or forgetting , depending on your woke levels ) . <p> Winston Peters reiterates his ' war on woke @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the words of wisdom from Winston Peters , saw more than a thousand people spill out of the main room of the Palmerston North Convention Centre onto the second floor level . There was a cinema-style trailer showing Peters walking through parliament , walking through protestors , walking through building sites and businesses , while reiterating his commitment to the " war on woke " . " Wow , " whispered voices in the crowd . " Wow . " <p> Winston Peters on the podium ( Photo : Lyric Waiwiri-Smith ) . <p> It ai n't been easy , Peters told his people , with accusations of the party being xenophobic ( " they love that word because it has a lot of vowels " ) and the threat of 50% of Aotearoa 's population consisting of immigrants within the next 15 years . But there 's a way out from bad times : smarter immigration policies , which would see NZ First demand that all new migrants sign up to a " Kiwi Values document " . If they 're not keen on that , " we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " . <p> But back to the culture wars . There was a list of issues to address , from climate change to Te Pati Maori to Covid-19 and those who see themselves as " it , they/them , and furry " ( which Peters pronounced as " fury " ) . No protestors managed to slink their way in , so the only boos heard were those reacting to the idea of transgender people using changing rooms or playing sport , or to Chl ? e Swarbrick being prime minister . " And in the words of Starship , nothing is gon na stop us now , " Peters grinned . The exit music was Ray Charles ' ' Hit the Road Jack ' . <p> A rare win for the non-binary community <p> What ate up the largest chunk of the convention were debates over the 55 proposed NZ First remits . On Saturday , the first 21 remits were passed , including pledges to set an end date for the Waitangi Tribunal , investigating the removal of Round Up in urban areas , reestablishing the Jobs for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Nuclear Fusion . <p> A reminder to engage in polite and productive debate over the remits went down like a lead balloon with the crowd , of which the median age was those who are eligible for a SuperGold card . If you wanted a turn to speak , you could raise the mini New Zealand flag provided for you at your table , or loudly mutter away in your seat until someone paid attention to you . <p> The NZ First members raise their flags to vote . ( Photo : Lyric Waiwiri-Smith ) <p> By Sunday , members were deliberating over a policy to " establish funding for a health check for all women at the age of 45 " -- the immediate problem with this one being , as identified by the women in the crowd , that it 's too exclusive . After all , who 's thinking about the need for prostate cancer checks ? " You want to delete ' women ' ? " emcee Julian Paul asked . Yes , the crowd responded , and now the approved remit reads : " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over the age of 45 " -- a rare win for the non-binary community . <p> Members did n't get through all 55 remits , with the 30th -- " encourage and actively promote adoption " -- dragging things out a bit . It was an important policy , minister Mark Patterson reckoned , because it 'd address those niggly issues that no one really knows how to fix head-on , like upping our birth rates and lightly discouraging abortion . The crowd asked some hard questions in response : does n't this wording still promote abortion ? What 's to be done about " children being promoted abortion " at schools ? And will this actually fix our birth rates ? <p> So , after a lot of back and forth , the remit is now : " support adoption as an option for informed decision-making " . <p> Chris Trotter was off his rocker <p> A wistful Chris Trotter , **32;298;TOOLONG , gave a rousing but outr ? mid-morning speech , which managed to hit all the populist talking points within 30 minutes . He warned of threats @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Graham Linehan , punished " for the crime of upsetting people online " ( the court was told Linehan had " relentlessly " posted abusive comments about a teenage transgender campaigner ) -- as well as a " conspiracy of magicians who can transform into men and women with a puff of a pronouns " , and the benefits of Trumpism and New Zealand First , which maybe are n't so different : they both value nostalgic times , and they 're both " daggers in the heart " of neoliberalism . <p> The merchandise available for the supporters . @@129020608 <h> Missing targets : Govt risks falling behind in four key areas <p> For a full explanation of each target and how it has changed , see RNZ 's target-by-target breakdown below . <p> Four of the government 's nine key targets risk not being met , the latest update shows . <p> People receiving Jobseeker benefits have increased significantly to 216,000 and education targets remain elusive . A target to reduce the number of households in emergency accommodation has been met , as has a goal to reduce the number of victims of assault or robbery . <p> RNZ is tracking progress towards each of the government 's nine targets , using official data from the relevant agencies . The graphics are updated each quarter , with the most recent progress report covering up to June 2025 . <p> Employment and education goals - in reading , writing and mathematics - are classified as " at risk " of not being met , and two health targets are considered " feasible " : still possible , but behind schedule and facing " major risks and/or issues " . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of households in emergency housing , cutting crime and youth offending , and meeting near-term greenhouse gas emission targets . Officials also say lifting school attendance is " probable " . <p> The nine targets were selected to focus the public sector on priorities . <p> Progress is reported quarterly , and each responsible agency assigns a status , ranging from " on track " to " unachievable " . Progress toward reaching a target can still be classed as " feasible " , even if there are major risks or issues in meeting it , as long as the agency in charge believes these can be resolved . <p> The nine targets are set to be delivered by 2030 , beyond the current political term . <h> Health <p> The goal for 95 percent of patients to be admitted , discharged or transferred from an emergency department within six hours remains far from reach . <p> The latest period of reporting shows only 74.2 percent of patients were seen within that timeframe - a slight rise from 72.1 percent in the previous quarter . <p> When setting this target @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be achievable in the short term . <p> " Most ED emergency departments nation-wide are over capacity most of the time , " a briefing to ministers read . <p> Wait times are affected by resourcing , community services , bed availability and seasonal pressures such as increased flu outbreaks . Reducing them would require " significant system-wide change " across hospitals , primary care and aged care , officials said . <p> They also warned against diverting too many resources to EDs at the expense of other services : " This may result in improved ED wait times in the short term , but - through reduction in the quality of care elsewhere - would likely result in worse health outcomes and ultimately higher ED presentations in the medium to long term . " <p> The update noted that $164 million over four years was announced in the 2025 Budget to expand urgent and after-hours care . <p> The target for 95 percent of people to receive elective treatment within four months is a long way away from being achieved , with performance slipping in the past quarter . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ treatments , such as hip or cataract surgeries , are seen within four months . This is slightly higher than the 59.2 percent reported in the last quarter . <p> An " elective boost " of funding has delivered 12,000 elective procedures , with many outsourced to private providers . <p> Delivery of this target is considered feasible , indicating there are still major risks . <h> Crime <p> The number of serious and persistent youth offenders has fallen by four since last quarter , leaving the target of 900 or fewer offenders still 38 away . <p> For a youth offender to be classed as a serious or persistent offender they must have committed three or more offences in the past 12 months , with at least one of them having a maximum penalty of seven years ' imprisonment or more . <p> Hitting the targeted 15 percent reduction by 2030 is considered on track . <p> Bootcamps , improving response teams and locally-led initiatives and increased school attendance are listed as areas the government is focused on to reach the target . <p> The goal to reduce crime @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the 2030 deadline . <p> This target was kept as one of the nine government targets , despite officials suggesting it would be difficult to achieve and should be replaced with something easier to reach . <p> The goal to reduce the number of victims of assault , robbery , and sexual assault by 20,000 by 2030 is based on data from the New Zealand Crime and Victims ' Survey . Officials warned the survey data had a high margin of error and was more suitable for showing long-term trends . <p> The survey includes crimes that victims might have experienced up to two years prior . <p> Initiatives to reach the target include limiting sentencing discounts , improving security at targeted locations and breaking the cycle of violence with expanded drug and alcohol interventions for in remand custody . <h> Employment <p> The number of people receiving Jobseeker support has risen by 6,200 to 216,000 since the March report . This target remains classified as " at risk " of being met . <p> This is nearly 36,000 above the forecast and 76,000 away from the overall goal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The government 's update suggested that the flow of people receiving the Jobseeker benefit will decrease as economic conditions improve . <p> Measures to reduce the number of people on Jobseeker benefit include stricter sanctions via a traffic light system . The system saw 13,000 sanctions handed out in the latest quarter . <h> Education <p> At 65.9 percent , Term 1 's attendance rate is well below the target of 80 percent of students present for more than 90 percent of the term . This means students should take no more than five days off a term . <p> Absence is classed as either " justified " or " unjustified " . Justified absences include illnesses , and other reasons which fall under school policy , such as suspensions . Unjustified absences include truancy , or taking holidays in term time . The government target of 80 percent makes no distinction between the two . <p> Notes provided to Cabinet when the targets were being set said it would be difficult to meet the 80 percent target while Covid-19 is still circulating . <p> The Ministry of Education @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " technical forecast " but is based on historical patterns of greater attendance drops during winter months due to illness . At present attendance is tracking closely to the ministry 's estimation . <p> Since 2011 , the highest percentage of students attending 90 percent of a school term was 72.8 percent , in Term 1 of 2019 . The average over that time was 59.4 percent . <p> Reaching this target is deemed " probable " . <p> Achievement rates for mathematics , reading and writing are still well below the government target of 80 percent of Year 8 students being at or above the expected curriculum level . <p> Currently 47 percent of students are at the expected level in reading , 24 percent in writing and 23 percent in mathematics . <p> A structured literacy programme has been rolled out , along with a refreshed curriculum . The latest report says this update offers a snapshot of levels prior the the rollout taking effect . <p> Mathematics and writing action plans have been launched to raise achievement . <p> This target is considered to be " at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Driving down the number of households in emergency housing is one target where progress leapt ahead of estimations . <p> From a baseline of 3141 households , the goal was to reduce the number by 75 percent , to 800 or fewer . <p> The current number is 501 , well below the 800 which was the 2030 goal . <p> Part of the plan to reach the target includes improving access to other forms of housing for emergency housing residents . As of 30 June , 1,041 households ( with 2,220 children ) have been housed in a social housing tenancy . Work is being done in Wellington and Waikato to move people out of emergency housing , and in December 2025 contracts with Rotorua motels will cease . <h> Climate <p> There are two targets New Zealand has committed to meeting as part of its net zero climate change goal . <p> The first target is for total greenhouse gas emissions between 2022 and 2025 to be below 290 megatonnes . This target is on track to being achieved . <p> Reaching the second target - for total @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mt - appears to be more of a challenge due to a project to store carbon facing uncertainty , as it does n't stack up commercially . This project represented a third of proposed carbon savings expected to be made from 2025 to 2030.

If you know where influence is being brought to bear , email us in confidence **29;679;TOOLONG <p> The Environmental Defence SocietyPart 1 examines a change of fortunes for the EDS , one of the country 's most successful advocacy groups , under this coalition <p> Part 2 asks how a small team led for decades by one man has achieved outsize influence on our environmental causes and policies <p> A litigation and policy-focused think tank built its success on long-term relationships and moderate pragmatism @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ well as the approval of conservationists . <p> Through litigation and lobbying for policy change , the Environmental Defence Society ( EDS ) has sought to benefit the natural world and everyone who relies on it . The question of ' who benefits ' in this case , according to the group , is ' everyone ' . <p> But its method of cementing environmental policy via litigation means that for every win EDS tallies , another party chalks up a loss . This strategy has put EDS up against mining companies , the agriculture industry and major infrastructure developers , whose projects have been derailed in the courts . <p> EDS prefers negotiation and targeted lawsuits to outright protest and disruption , a strategy that has kept it in the government 's tent for five decades . But this time-honoured approach hit an obstacle when the coalition Government took power , advancing a spate of development projects with environmental overrides not seen since the time of former prime minister Sir Robert Muldoon . <p> As society leader Gary Taylor fought to keep conservative ministers on side , all eyes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ student activists crashed the event . <h> read more <p> Climate Change Minister Simon Watts decided to head for the exit by the time activists unfurled their banners . <p> The protest , organised by School Strike 4 Climate , consisted of hand-painted messages and chants to stop the Government 's controversial fast-track for development projects . " EDS remains committed to maintaining that interface , sticking to its strategy of staying in the political tent no matter the political stripe of government , even if environmental groups are going the other way . <p> All of the major environmental advocacy groups have felt the financial impact of the coalition Government 's budget . But despite its relative reliance on government funding , according to annual returns filed with the Charities Service , EDS is the only one of the big four environmental NGOs to increase its revenue over the past two years . <p> Its revenues are well below those of Forest &amp; Bird and Greenpeace , but have started to close the gap with the World Wide Fund for Nature ( WWF ) . <p> Sometimes this money even moves laterally , as Forest &amp; Bird ( along with the Michael and Suzanne Borrin Foundation ) supported EDS @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ released on the 1st of September . <p> With a small member base , EDS is able to shrink in times of fiscal constraint . Its in-house experts continue pursuing litigation , while others take aim at policy research they predict will be attractive to the next government . But as public anger grows , traditional diplomacy is replaced with protest . <p> Former conservation minister Eugenie Sage says this Government 's tendency to push through legislation without public scrutiny or cooperation with NGOs means more people will abandon the diplomatic approach and turn to action . In her time , EDS 's reports were a source of in-depth research officials did n't have time to produce , she says . <p> " I think it reflects badly on the Government that it wo n't even listen to EDS , which has got a track record of focusing on policy and the substantive issues , and doing the analysis to justify its conclusions , and has been extremely moderate , " Sage says . <p> " What this Government is doing is forcing people into direct action like the coal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the opportunities for public participation . It is sneering at environmental-anyone who speaks out , demonising them . " <p> Environmental protests have shifted into a higher gear since the coalition Government was formed in 2023 . <p> Activists from 350 Aotearoa climbed into Bathurst 's coal buckets on the West Coast and , on August 22 , Greenpeace members mounted a bottom trawler in Nelson to unfurl an " Ocean Killer " banner . The activists were arrested within hours . <p> Protestors from 350 Aotearoa stopped coal exports from one branch of the Stockton Mine by climbing into the gondola 's buckets . Photo : supplied <p> Protest has never been the EDS way . It does n't have a wide membership and focuses its resources on litigation and shaping public policy . <p> The strategy is n't foolproof , however . <p> Victory in the courts can be temporary when governments have the power to change the law -- and do so , sometimes , after consulting profit-making vested interests . <p> After the landmark King Salmon case , decided by the Supreme Court in 2014 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Act ( RMA ) completely unworkable and cease all development . Lobbyists attempted to pressure the government to remove the word " avoid " from the act 's the crucial line , and EDS fought to keep the ground it had won . ( More on King Salmon in part two . ) <p> A decade later , something similar happened with freshwater . In recent years , a new litigation group called Environmental Law Initiative has had a series of significant court wins . Two of the eventual rulings formed a sort of dual pincer on freshwater pollution that Newsroom has reported on previously . <p> Once again , critics warned the implications of these rulings meant the RMA could become unworkable , and lobbied the Government . Where EDS was able to keep the ground it won , the Environmental Law Initiative could n't . The RMA has been changed to skirt around the implications of the rulings , in line with the requests by private interest groups . <p> Gary Taylor and Shay Schlaepfer , then an in-house lawyer for EDS , on a visit to Waipuka @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on the ground to hear concerns from locals about projects in their area . Photo : Raewyn Peart <p> However , if neither of those approaches -- creating policy to make better laws or enforcing existing laws in court -- are successful , things can be changed on the ground . <p> Rewinding the clock to 1984 , EDS was in what appeared to be a losing battle to stop an open-cast gold mine in the Coromandel . The new Labour government had granted prospecting permits , and EDS 's judicial review of the High Court proceedings failed . It withdrew its proceedings and faced defeat . <p> Case Study : Goldmines New ZealandGoldmines New Zealand , a subsidiary of the Anglo American Corporation of South Africa , sought to open an open-cast gold mine on the Coromandel Peninsula in 1981 . Its target was $1.2 billion worth of gold , and it lodged applications for three prospecting licenses covering an area of 2900 hectares . ' EDS initially objected to these licenses along with locals , who had coalesced under the flag of Peninsula Watchdog . The judge in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Patterson , a former mines inspector who was born and raised on the West Coast . Tony Randerson -- more on him in Part 2 -- led the case for EDS.Patterson ruled he would not hear evidence from the public on the impacts of the proposed mine , since the applications in question were for prospecting only . But at the time , under the Mining Act 1971 , prospecting licenses automatically rolled into a mining license , meaning this was the only opportunity for debate.EDS sought a review of this ruling by the High Court , which sided with EDS and ordered Patterson to stop his investigation until the High Court resolved the challenge . Patterson refused to do so until he saw a hard copy of the document . As Randerson began to say it would be there that afternoon , Taylor arrived with a copy in hand , halting proceedings.The High Court ruled Patterson had to consider the impacts of mining as there would be no other chance to do so , a decision which would later contribute to the removal of the " automatic right to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do the right thing " in the area , but could n't provide specifics on the mine itself . Ultimately , Patterson granted two of its three licenses.EDS continued to lobby for changes to the Mining Act , and had the automatic rights provision removed in 1982 . EDS lawyers drafted the Thames-Coromandel District Council Mining Moratorium Bill , which was set to enact an 18-month freeze on mining licenses . But as the gears of policy slowly turned , Muldoon 's July 1984 snap election changed the political landscape.In December of that year , new Energy Minister Bob Tizard decided to grant all three prospecting licenses to Goldmines New Zealand.EDS filed for judicial review of the proceedings behind this decision , under the impression that Tizard had not consulted with the Minister of Lands . But when Tizard produced an affidavit saying that the consultation was properly conducted , EDS conceded the case . <p> EDS failed to win via changing the Mining Act 1971 , and failed to win via enforcing judicial review . But the mine never opened because the public stepped up . <p> Peninsula Watchdog @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up the miner 's toilets and chucked dead possums in their water tanks . Their relentless tactics stretched to slashing tyres and stealing keys . The mining company called it quits in 1985 . <p> Local voices have been a focus of Taylor 's over the course of his career . His stance on the government 's ability to override local opinion is best exemplified by this passage : <p> " For Parliament to legislate to override the general law of the land , and more particularly , the fruits of litigation by affected landowners , is a very grave step indeed . It is constitutionally unacceptable and undermines the rule of law . How can affected parties oppose ill considered projects in the future through the judicial process with any confidence ? It encourages extralegal approaches to opposition , and is a matter that government should reflect upon . You can not give people rights and then take them away because you do n't like the result . " <p> Taylor wrote this excerpt from an EDS annual report more than 40 years ago . It was referencing Muldoon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , prioritising economic growth over the voices of local landowners . Four decades later , in 2025 , Taylor says " I think it 's still pertinent . " <p> -- <p> EDS and the country 's other environmental NGOs work together , their leaders acknowledge . <p> Each has a part in pursuing a shared goal , even if their methods do n't align . Faced with a blizzard of changes to environmental policy and regulations , including cuts to public legal aid funding , Greenpeace prefers to protest . EDS , meanwhile , hunkers down , focusing on policy and while trusting its reputation and small size will allow it to weather the storm . <p> Taylor says : " We operate in a narrower realm , and do that quite deliberately in order to keep focused and muster our limited resources towards focused outcomes , focused methods . " <p> Former Green Party co-leader Russel Norman , who heads Greenpeace Aotearoa , says EDS is an advocacy organisation . " It either puts more pressure on the government not to do these terrible things , or if there 's a change in government somewhere along the line , it creates a mandate to do something different . " <p> WWF-New Zealand chief executive Kayla Kingdon-Bebb , a former policy director at the Department of Conservation , says her organisation also prefers to remain in the policy-making tent -- to a point . <p> " You 're in the tent for a reason . You 're there to provide constructive feedback to help the government of the day make the best decisions possible for the people of New Zealand . And if it becomes apparent that you 're only in the tent so that they can say you 're in the tent , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ perhaps it 's time to go on a different camping trip . " <p> But Taylor hopes the approach will prove successful in the long run . " I think it 's not too late for the Government to ratchet back to some sort of more responsible ground , where environment and development are seen as mutually compatible objectives , rather than one being seen as a threat to the other . " <p> Taylor 's track record has shown a preference for negotiation and a belief common ground can be found . Still , EDS has met defeat before , and ultimately ca n't change the minds of decision-makers committed to a certain outcome . <p> But Taylor 's influence has survived decades of turnover . He 's older than most of our bedrock environmental laws , with connections to ministers longer than some of their colleagues have been alive . He says he was one of the first people in the Beehive after ministers took office . <p> If there was a question of personal benefit for Taylor , that claim would speak to it . His work @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sense ; the man has lived in the same rural home for decades . Rather , his work -- and Peart 's , and Severinsen 's , and all the others -- with EDS has benefitted them by granting reputation and influence . " EDS has been doing this 50 years , right ? They 're not going to pivot tomorrow , just as the people in coal buckets have been going up in coal buckets and tying themselves to trees for 50 years . There 's a place for all of that , and it resonates with different parts of society .
