@@129409614 <p> A standoff between New Zealand and the Cook Islands reflects an urgent question in the Pacific : Is China a threat or a partner for growth ? <p> Every year , tens of thousands of tourists visit the pristine beaches and turquoise lagoons of the Cook Islands in the South Pacific . Their business sustainsthis small nation of 15 islands and atolls , where no building is taller than a coconut tree . <p> Most travellers come from New Zealand , the Cook Islands ' former colonial ruler , and the two have long had close ties . But in recent months , they have been locked in an icy standoff . <p> The fallout was over a wide-ranging partnership agreement the Cook Islands signed this year with China . The deal , viewed as the latest sign of China 's encroachment in the region , has alarmed New Zealand . <p> New Zealand has put on hold millions of dollars in aid , leaving a hole in the Cook Islands ' budget . Prime Minister Mark Brown of the Cook Islands has threatened to " go somewhere else @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been accusations of New Zealand using aid as a bargaining chip , and rare protests in Rarotonga , the main island , by people concerned about jeopardising ties with New Zealand . <p> Even after the two countries ' leaders met face to face last week at a regional forum , there has been no sign of a breakthrough . <p> " It was a serious change when they entered those arrangements with China , and we knew nothing of it , " New Zealand 's Foreign Minister , Winston Peters , said in an interview last month . <p> New Zealand argues that the Cook Islands was obligated to consult with it before signing the deal , and says there will be no new aid until steps are taken to " repair the relationship and restore trust " . Brown has pointed out that New Zealand signed its own partnership agreement with China in 2014 -- without consulting the Cook Islands -- reaping tens of billions of dollars in economic gain . <p> But in the decade since that agreement , New Zealand has grown increasingly wary of China @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ China used coercive trade measures to hamstring Australia 's economy , test-fired an intercontinental ballistic missile into the Pacific , and sent a naval task force into the waters between Australia and New Zealand to carry out live fire drills . <p> Peters said China 's deal with the Cook Islands " fits a certain recent developmental pattern " of Beijing 's ambitions in the region . <p> Diplomats from the Cook Islands and New Zealand were " working their way through " the former 's agreement with China to work out points of concern , New Zealand officials said . <p> The paused funding -- worth $18.2 million -- was for health , education and tourism ; a separate $3 million in aid for cyber security is still being provided , according to New Zealand 's Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade . <p> Analysts said the pause in aid was an extraordinary move by New Zealand . It is a different tack from Australia , which has dramatically increased aid spending in the region . <p> Since 1965 , the Cook Islands has been self-governing , but relies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Zealand passports and the majority of them -- nearly 85,000 -- live in New Zealand . The parameters of their relationship are being tested as China has deepened its presence in the Pacific . <p> In 2022 , China signed a security agreement with the Solomon Islands , another small Pacific nation , heightening fears that Beijing was aiming to establish a military foothold in the region . Both Chinese and Cook Islands officials have said their pact does not include security or military matters . <p> New Zealand 's intelligence chief , in a speech this year , warned that China 's ambition in making inroads into the Pacific was to " link economic and security co-operation , create competing regional architectures " . China 's embassy in Wellington called his comments disinformation , saying there is no " secret agenda " to the Cook Islands agreement . <p> David Capie , director of the Centre for Strategic Studies at Victoria University of Wellington , said it was clear that New Zealand 's objections had to do with Beijing signalling increased military ambitions in the region . <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ With different partners , that was probably fine , " he said . " The strategic context has changed . " <p> The Cook Islands , one of the smallest of the Pacific island nations , has also been a focal point of emerging competition over seabed mining . The agreement with China included pledges to co-operate on exploring seabed minerals . The United States last month reached its own deal with the Cook Islands on mining the sea floor . <p> Rashneel Kumar , editor of Cook Island News , which broke the news of New Zealand 's funding pause , said that , like other small Pacific island nations with developmental challenges and climate change risks , his country needed support wherever it could get it . All around Avarua , the capital , are physical reminders of this -- the courthouse , the police headquarters , the national stadium , each built by China . <p> " The feeling is there that China has helped us in some areas and we are grateful for that , " he said . Nearly all of the prize tasks ( with four notable exceptions ) <p> The prize tasks that kick off every episode of Taskmaster are but a tepid amuse-bouche to the piping hot buffet carnage to come . Sure , it 's a nice chance to get to know our contestants better , but there 's a level of forward-planning here that feels dull and calculated compared to the spontaneity of the rest of the episode . With that said , our panel found three prize tasks that cracked the top 50 . But which ones ? You 'll have to keep reading ( hehe ) . / AC <p> Spoiler alert : Jack Ansett 's prize task is one . <p> 161 . @@129449481 <p> Former National Party Finance Minister Ruth Richardson has called on her successor , Nicola Willis , to pass an emergency austerity budget by Christmas in response to the June swoon . <p> Willis would do well to ignore her . Doctor ( honorary ) Richardson made up her mind 35 years ago and would prescribe the same medicine regardless of the symptoms . <p> Her 1991 budget intensified a downturn for over two years . Unemployment surged to 11% , poverty levels shot up and never fully recovered , protestors took to the streets , and New Zealand 's social fabric frayed . <p> She would argue the fiscal situation was so serious that this pain was unavoidable and strong economic growth between 1993 and 1996 had proven it all worthwhile . She may well be right . <p> But let 's not overstate the case . Much of that growth was making up for the years of recession , the global economy was in an upswing , and the New Zealand economy had freshly liberalised . <p> Today the situation is very different . Public debt is a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are roughly half what they were in the 1990s . New Zealand has a near-perfect credit rating with a stable outlook , compared to being at risk of its third downgrade back then . <p> Richardson inherited an economy which had just been radically liberalised . It was not just spending cuts which built confidence . It was also an independent central bank , a floating currency , and a true market economy flourishing for the first time . <p> Setting aside the ethical debates about deregulation , there simply is n't much low-hanging productivity fruit for politicians to pick and the Coalition is already reforming what it can . <p> What would New Zealand gain by slashing spending ? It would not improve our credit rating , attract any foreign capital , or give households and businesses more confidence . <p> It would simply prolong the economic downturn while greatly improving the chances of the Labour Party winning the next election and immediately reversing the austerity measures anyway . <p> New Zealand is not Argentina . There Javier Milei has had some success with radical neoliberal reforms and spending @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ country ' in cooee ' of what New Zealand already is : a successful , liberal , solvent economy . <p> Slashing spending today would undoubtedly delay economic recovery . I asked ChatGPT if there is any case where austerity has worked after the neoliberal budgets of the 1990s . Its answer is : <p> Since 2000 , there are examples where countries implemented large fiscal consolidations and later saw bigger economies or higher GDP per person ( Estonia , Latvia , Iceland ; Germany and Ireland are more complex cases ) . But in every case the positive outcome was conditional -- not a simple causal law that " austerity alone makes an economy bigger and more productive . " Most credible research finds success requires complementary policies ( bank repair , reforms , favourable external environment ) and good timing . <p> I then asked how many it did n't work in : <p> About 25 -- 30 OECD and EU countries attempted @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in listening to Ruth Richardson . It brought federal spending down from a peak of 44 percent of gross national @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 1948 , a drop of over 35 percentage points of GNP . <p> While government spending fell like a stone , federal tax revenues fell only a little , from a peak of $44.4 billion in 1945 to $39.7 billion in 1947 and $41.4 billion in 1948 . In other words , from peak to trough , tax revenues fell by only $4.7 billion , or 10.6 percent . Yet , the economy boomed . The unemployment rate , which was artificially low at the end of the war because many millions of workers had been drafted into the U.S. armed services , did increase . But during the years from 1945 to 1948 , it reached its peak at only 3.9 percent in 1946 , and , for the months from September 1945 to December 1948 , the average unemployment rate was only 3.5 percent . Where Britain -- and most of the West -- had high levels of taxation , government spending , deficit financing , industrial planning and economic intervention , Hong Kong went the other way . Most people -- ' all but the well-to-do ' -- paid no income tax at all . Even higher earners only paid 15 per cent . There were no tariffs or duties , no sales taxes or VAT , no taxes on capital gains , on interest or on overseas earnings . But there was a land value tax . @@129449486 <p> Since 2009 New Zealand has spent $339 million into research on reducing livestock methane emissions , and the Government recently dedicated $155 million more , when at a global level any emission reductions achieved will be quickly cancelled out by much larger and increasing fossil fuel-sourced methane emissions . <p> It is critically important to understand that the correct assessment of the effects of greenhouse gas emissions is at the global level , not the national level . This is because what is released in New Zealand or anywhere else mixes in the atmosphere , travels around the world , and affects the entire planet . <p> When designing livestock emission reduction research , the impacts of other methane sources must be considered to ensure that what can be practically achieved with livestock will actually result in a net global reduction in methane emissions . All Nationally Determined Contributions ( NDC ) to the Paris Climate Agreement should be framed this way so that permanent , not pyrrhic emission reductions are achieved by those primarily responsible for them . <p> Fossil fuel methane emissions under-reported by 80% <p> It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ under-reported by 80% for decades ( IEA 2025 ) . Observations of under-reporting started to appear in research articles from 1996 in a local situation ( Scheff P A et al 1999 ; and in 1999 at a national level ( Subak S 1999 , Science Direct ) . A recent update is here at Cenci and Biffis 2025 . <p> If we add 80% more methane emissions to total energy emissions reported in 2023 we see that they exceeded those of agriculture by 110% ( Figure 2 below , 2023 Adjusted ) . This fact totally confounded any efforts by the agriculture industry to reduce global emissions of methane in 2023 . <p> Add 10% more net emissions for agriculture ( increasing food demand ) , and a further 56% growth in energy emissions to 2034 ( Figure 2 ) , we see that fossil fuel methane emissions will exceed livestock emissions by 150% in future , thoroughly cancelling out any livestock emission reductions achieved anywhere in the world . Thus , attempts to reduce livestock emissions may be heroic , but are ultimately futile . <p> Figure 2 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ International Energy Agency has been documenting under-reporting of methane emissions , and in its latest 2025 report , it notes that : <p> " Around 70% of methane emissions from the fossil fuel sector could be avoided with existing technologies , often at a low cost .... The agriculture and waste sectors are also major sources of methane emissions , but fossil fuel supply offers the greatest potential for immediate reductions in methane emissions . Solutions that lower methane emissions from fossil fuels to near zero already exist , and they could be deployed today at little -- or even negative -- cost . " <p> Simple house-keeping by the fossil-fuel industry could reduce their methane emissions by 70% , or 84 million tonnes per year ( 2025 figures ) . They could even make a dollar out of it by being more efficient ( IEA 2025 Global Methane Tracker ) . And it would n't cost farmers or taxpayers a cent . <p> Natural emissions from livestock since 12,000 years ago never affected climate <p> Going back a bit , agriculture began to develop during the Neolithic Revolution around @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fuels were discovered , and lived within the natural environment for 12 millennia without their natural emissions causing any dangerous climate change . With the growing world population and the expanse of human civilisation , livestock numbers increased and they became the primary source of food protein for the world and the foundation of the New Zealand economy . <p> Figure 3 : Growth in Livestock Populations 1890-2014 <p> Livestock methane emissions have increased about 8.5-fold ( from a very small initial amount ) as livestock numbers grew to feed the increasing world population . <p> Rocketing fossil fuel emissions over past 175 years <p> There is a spectacularly different picture for fossil fuels . Widespread use of coal , oil and gas grew rapidly during the Industrial Revolution from the 1850 's onwards . The first public observation in New Zealand that this could alter climate was expressed in the Rodney and Otamatea Times on 14 August 1912 by an unknown author . <p> Rodney and Otamatea Times 14 August 1912 <p> The article was prophetic . Since 1850 there has been a 13-fold jump in emissions from fossil @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and a 1.5 ? C increase in global temperature . An assessment by the European Union in 2024 shows that agriculture ( all types ) contributes about 12% of global emissions , fossil fuels 87% . <p> Figure 4 : Global Emissions by Sector EU 2024 <p> Antarctica - the gigantic bellwether of dangerous climate change <p> The Arctic and Antarctic have been described as the two " anchors " of climate on Earth . Sea ice follows a pattern of melting in summer and refreezing in winter . Because of warming waters and a warming atmosphere , the Arctic is now ice-free in the summer , so one anchor is away . <p> Antarctica is the fifth-largest continent . It is about 1.5 times the size of the United States and nearly twice the size of Australia . It holds 90% of all freshwater on Earth in 30 million cubic metres of ice ( 2020 reference ) . <p> Sea ice measurements in Antarctica began in 1978 . From 2016 onwards , Antarctic sea ice entered a new ' low-extent " regime , where large areas of ice did @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . East Antarctica started melting first , and then from 2022 the much larger West Antarctica icesheet started breaking up ( Doddridge , E. et al 2025 ) . Livestock populations have not rocketed up sufficiently since 1978 to drive this melting , but fossil fuel-sourced emissions have . <p> Fossil fuel emissions ( all greenhouse gases ) are five to seven times that of livestock emissions . Even if all agriculture on Earth stopped , there would still be a climate change problem . Huge pieces of Antarctica are not falling off because of relatively small levels of methane emissions from cows and sheep . <p> It 's not us <p> New Zealand disadvantaged by a " false negative " emissions profile <p> Livestock emissions would not be an issue for New Zealand if it did not have a unique emissions profile dominated by agricultural emissions at 53% of the total ( 2024 data ) . Most developed countries have a profile dominated by energy emissions from fossil fuels . Because New Zealand has already decarbonised its energy production with 85% of it coming from renewable electricity , a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ appear to be a climate laggard . <p> Ironically , New Zealand is being penalised for its global leadership in clean energy in an agriculture-based economy . Other nations can " look better " than New Zealand if they decarbonise their fossil fuel dominated energy production and claim this as an achievement , when New Zealand has already mostly decarbonised its energy production . <p> Figure 5 : Energy Production from Fossil Fuels by Selected Countries <p> Nevertheless , New Zealand and other agriculturally-based countries have been under pressure to reduce livestock emissions as part of their Paris Climate Agreement commitments . Research on agricultural greenhouse gas emissions began in New Zealand in 2007 , with New Zealand leading the formation of the Global Research Alliance on Agricultural Greenhouse Gases in 2009 . The GRA has 68 countries and 20 partners contributing to over 90 research projects . <p> Research efforts include developing feed additives , breeding low-emitting animals , improved feed efficiency , methane-reducing vaccination and methane inhibitors that reduce the production of methane in the animals ' multi-stomach digestive systems . Notably in 2024 the GRA promoted a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cows , sheep , goats etc ) digestion systems , which are incredibly complex . The further $155 million in funding over the next five years is targeted at the reduction of livestock emissions . So far , research results project an expected 10% reduction in livestock emissions at some time in the future . <p> None of this appears to have been considered in the global context of methane emissions from multiple sources , which are the most dangerous , and which are the most effective and cheapest to reduce . <p> Remarkably , the academic authors of the June 2025 Open Letter to the New Zealand Prime Minister chastising him about New Zealand 's " no additional warming " policy seem to have forgotten or are unaware of these four critical facts : <p> 1 ) The basic science of global mixing of greenhouse gases ; <p> 2 ) New Zealand 's emissions profile is dominated by agricultural emissions because it has already mostly decarbonised its energy sector ; <p> 3 ) Fossil fuel-sourced methane emissions cancel out any reductions in livestock emissions and will continue to do @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 4 ) It is easy and cheap , even profitable , for the fossil fuel industry to greatly reduce its methane emissions by good house-keeping <p> Mr Luxon rightly rejected the Open Letter allegations , telling the authors to turn their attention to other countries with higher-emissions agriculture . A better rebuttal would have been to state that : <p> " Fossil fuel methane emissions have always greatly exceeded livestock emissions and will continue to do so for the foreseeable future , so there is no justification for spending taxpayer 's money on pointless research into futile and economically damaging livestock emission reduction strategies . Methane emissions from fossil fuels should be targeted instead . " <p> Unfortunately Ministry for Primary Industry officials did not give this type of advice to the Prime Minister . They also appear to have failed to keep up with the 29-year history of fossil fuel methane emission under-reporting and its fundamental impact on livestock methane emissions policy ( a 30-minute literature search for them and the Open Letter authors ) . Nor have they appeared to have considered whether or not the predicted 10% @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at a higher cost to taxpayers ( $85-$113 per tonne with $339 million ) than NZU carbon credits ( currently $58 per unit ) , will actually contribute to a net global reduction in methane emissions . Or whether the further $155 million ( grand total $123-$165 per tonne ) will be value for money for New Zealand . <p> In short , no . <p> You have been poorly advised , Mr Prime Minister , sir <p> But they were all deceived ..... <p> Nobody seems to have highlighted these simple methane emission facts on the international stage , nor at International Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) meetings . Instead , it appears that most academics and politicians have been taken in by a cynical carbon-con which holds that livestock emissions are contributing significantly to dangerous climate change and that research needs to be done to reduce those emissions for the good of the planet , gas-lighting the agricultural sector . Even the IPCC has been misled ( as we see from the Open Letter ) , since the reduction of livestock emissions is included in Paris @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fuel-sourced emissions are the real ( and easy-to-fix ) methane problem . <p> Bad advice leads to bad policy and law and bad outcomes . There have even been absurd suggestions that farmers should destock to reduce methane emissions , reducing world protein supplies and farmer incomes . Unnecessary and futile destocking will create significant losses for food producers and consumers in the first and second worlds , and will make subsistence farmers in third world countries even poorer . <p> Who will require African herders to destock and starve their families ? <p> Why is the whole world off-track with livestock emissions ? <p> Suffocating levels of fossil fuel industry propaganda continue to deceive and misdirect the world audience with deceptive messaging on the actual existence and causes of dangerous climate change . A mixture of part truths ( depending on context ) , and out-right lies are presented : <p> Fossil fuel development is well-established , will meet your future energy needs , is sustainable , and will make you rich <p> Clean energy ca n't do what fossil fuels do <p> Clean energy technologies do n't work @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and will take too long to change to clean energy <p> Climate change is a hoax and not that bad anyway <p> One example of how effective this lobbying is can be seen with the 2021 Global Methane Pledge , where New Zealand is a signatory . Methane abatement commitments under the Pledge include 195 nations and cover 80% of oil and gas emissions . However , no meaningful reductions have occurred in the past four years . The fossil fuel industry continues to exert its extraordinary lobbying power globally , with more industry lobbyists at the 2024 COP29 ( annual IPCC climate conference ) in Baku , Azerbaijan , than representatives from scientific institutions , indigenous communities and vulnerable nations ( Mongabay 2024 ) . It is not surprising that fossil-fuel industry pitches and influence on politicians are delaying effective climate action . <p> The fossil fuel industry is a sunset industry under pressure for its very survival this century . It is conducting a desperate but effective rear-guard action to maintain its dominance over nations and governments because clean energy is now significantly cheaper than dirty energy ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fuel emissions are driving dangerous climate change . <p> China and Germany are the world 's most prominent investors and producers of renewable energy solutions . Their technologies are having global impact releasing affordable clean energy . Scientists have established a causal link between energy company emissions and US$ 28 trillion in climate damage , possibly exposing energy companies to legal action . In Europe last summer , 16,500 deaths were attributed to climate change-induced extreme heat ( Bloomberg 17.09.25 ) . Oil prices are going below break-even level of $USD60 per barrel as society transits away from fossil fuels . These are all existential threats to fossil fuel-based energy companies . <p> It 's a pity that they are not using their gigantic technical and financial resources to lead the transition to clean energy , which after all is better for their customers and staff* , less likely to attract litigation , and would ensure their business ' sustainability for the rest of the 21st Century and into the 22nd . <p> New Zealand leads the 68-nation Global Research Alliance on Agricultural Greenhouse Gases . Now that it is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ futile , it is morally bound to advise the GRA to refocus on agricultural climate change adaptation research . This new direction should concentrate on providing solutions for farmers having to adapt to droughts , floods , heat stress on animals , and less pasture production among other things . For example , scientists and engineers could be re-directed to work on water harvesting , drought-resistant pasture types , soil health improvement with less water , super-efficient irrigation systems , accurate forecasting of extreme weather events , clean energy power on-farm ( eg. solar ) , retrofitting vehicles and machinery to run on clean energy sources , and so forth . <p> A parallel better investment for New Zealand would be to develop some diplomatic bottle and lead the 68 nations into lobbying the IPCC to remove natural livestock emissions from the Paris Climate Agreement . Then the world could focus more effectively on reducing the true sources of dangerous climate change and pay no attention to those who want to keep putting out the fire with gasoline , or who claim there is no fire . @@129485656 <h> Government passes adoption bill under urgency <p> Urgency is a tool the government can use to speed up the progress of legislation . File picture . Photo : RNZ / Angus Dreaver <p> Parliament passed an immediate change to New Zealand 's adoption laws this week , progressing the Adoption Amendment Bill through all stages after it was added to the government 's urgency agenda at short notice . <p> Urgency is a tool the government can use to speed up the progress of legislation . It allows longer sitting days and skips the usual stand-down periods between the different stages of a bill . It also allows a bill to skip the select committee process . <p> Under Parliament 's Standing Orders , the government must explain why urgency is being used , and will usually outline which bills will be debated as part of it . <p> According to Leader of the House Chris Bishop , who requested the urgency , the Adoption Amendment Bill needed immediate progression to " prevent adoptions from proceeding where the safety of children could be at risk , and to ensure @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Royal assent , as applications would otherwise take a significant time to process once the legislation is enacted . " <p> The law change itself is intended as a temporary fix , in response to what the government says are currently unsafe adoption settings . The minister in charge of the bill , Nicole McKee , said during the bill 's first reading that the law did not have enough protections and because of this , " some children and young people adopted from overseas end up facing neglect , abuse , or exploitation here in New Zealand " . <p> The Adoption Amendment Bill was n't originally on the House 's agenda this week . While the government gave the opposition some notice of their intent to change the law , opposition MPs had only a short time to prepare , receiving a copy of the bill on Tuesday morning for a Tuesday afternoon debate . <p> Protecting young people from exploitation is not a contentious issue , so all parties supported the bill 's intent . But even when a bill 's intention has unanimous support , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and efficacy . <p> Perhaps nowhere is a more ample opportunity for this than during the Committee of the Whole House stage , where a bill is picked apart section by section , and MPs can quiz the minister in detail . <p> Given the short notice , opposition MPs likely had to skim-read the bill and respond to what was in front of them on the page . So instead of finger-pointing and grilling the minister , the committee stage was more of a candid examination of the legislative intent . <p> Committee stages often feature a familiar cast of Parliament 's former jurists and practitioners of detail . On this occasion , MPs like Labour 's Vanushi Walters and Duncan Webb , and the Greens ' Lawrence Xu-Nan were asking about the definitions of the terminology that appear frequently in the bill . <p> " ' The bill does not apply to overseas adoptions within the scope of the Hague Convention , ' " noted Webb . " That 's what the explanatory note says , but then , if you go to clause 4 , ' Interpretation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Convention adoption as ' an adoption to which the Hague Convention applies that took place in a contracting State in respect of which the adopted child was habitually resident overseas and in respect of which the adopted parent is , or both adopted parents are , habitually resident in New Zealand . ' ... In terms of the recognition of overseas adoption , I 'm just a little confused . Basically , does it mean that we do or do n't recognise adoptions where all of the parties at the time of the adoption were resident overseas ? " <p> The absence of political point-scoring made for a palpably more collegial affair than is sometimes the case at the committee stage ; less of a cross-examination , and more of a legislative focus group . <p> In response to Webb 's line of inquiry around definitions , McKee conceded that she too had experienced confusion . <p> " I 've spent a little bit of time myself trying to get it into my head about how this will operate , but effectively , the suspension is going to apply to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Now there 's a thing here about the wording . I originally had wanted to use those that were ' ordinarily resident ' but actually got told I ca n't use that word - or I should n't use that word - because ' habitually resident ' works better and there is a definition for that in other legislation , so we have used habitually resident . " <p> In the game of politics , where questions are so often loaded and answers are so often avoided , MPs may well have found it refreshing to experience a relatively productive and cordial committee stage , with Lawrence Xu-Nan , in the third and final reading , expressing his appreciation for the minister 's engagement and honesty during the committee stage . <h> Other bills under urgency this week <p> Following the completion of the Adoption Amendment , the government moved on to four first readings : <p> The Defence ( Workforce ) Amendment Bill , which streamlines the process of armed forces personnel filling in for civilian staff when said staff are participating in industrial action . This bill @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Committee for consideration and is currently taking submissions . <p> The Local Government ( Auckland Council ) ( Transport Governance ) Amendment Bill , which creates an Auckland Transport committee that will have equal representation of councillors and ministerial appointees . This bill is also now open for submissions . <p> The Retail Payment System ( Ban on Merchant Surcharges ) Amendment Bill restricts vendors from placing a surcharge on all PayWave transactions . It is now with the Finance and Expenditure Committee , which is currently taking submissions on the Bill . <p> The Income Tax ( FamilyBoost ) Amendment Bill , which was progressed through all stages and is pending Royal Assent , will soon be law . It expands the eligibility criteria and credit amount available through the FamilyBoost scheme . <p> The Customs ( Levies and Other Matters ) Amendment Bill , and the Climate Change Response ( Emissions Trading Scheme - Forestry Conversion ) Amendment Bill also passed through their remaining stages during urgency . <p> The House was in urgency working through all these bills until Wednesday evening , with the government returning to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> To listen to the audio version of this story , along with other happenings from Parliament this week , click the link near the top of the page . <p> * RNZ 's The House , with insights into parliament , legislation and issues , is made with funding from Parliament 's Office of the Clerk . Enjoy our articlesor podcastat RNZ.

@@129515483 <p> Niwa technician Steve Wilcox ( centre ) camped in the Antarctic after installing tidal stations at Possession Island , one of the first landing places in Antarctica . Photo : Supplied / Niwa <p> This story has been updated to show Steve Wilcox and Phil Jellyman also worked as Niwa regional managers . <p> Scientists are hopeful a supercharged weather forecaster will be one of the winners in the biggest overhaul of the science sector in 30 years . <p> Today marks a " new era for science " as six of the seven Crown Research Institutes ( CRI ) officially transition and merge into new Public Research Organisations ( PRO ) . <p> The mega science entities were heralded by the government as part of the major science reforms following Sir Peter Gluckman 's review of the science system , which also included the disestablishment of Callaghan Innovation - with funding for most of its functions signalled to end on 30 June . <p> The shake-up waves goodbye to Niwa and GNS , which now form the Earth Science Institute , and includes cherry-picked parts of Callaghan Innovation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ legislation for the PROs will follow in 2026 , which is when the science minister expects the fourth organisation , focused on advanced technologies , to be up-and-running . <p> The Association of Scientists says while it supports the mergers in principle , in the absence of significant funding , it 's sceptical the reforms can deliver systemic change for the sector . <p> While nerve-wracking for some staff , those leading the transition say they are excited by the opportunity for greater collaboration - and in line with government directives - the potential economic benefits . <h> Not the first shake-up for scientists <p> Niwa regional manager and Marine technician Steve Wilcox remembers a time when Niwa - known , among other things , for its marine and oceanographic science - had no research vessel . <p> He said in the first four years after it formed in 1992 , money was " amazingly tight " and the organisation was forced to charter ships - with the year on a Russian vessel proving memorable for its " fantastic characters , cultural challenges " , and unusual sewerage system . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ little rubbish bins beside the toilet which they emptied at three o'clock in the morning ... and the toilets were next door to the kitchen . " <p> It was not until 1996 when the Ministry of Fisheries research division merged with Niwa that ocean research kicked into gear , he said . <p> Wilcox said the merger granted Niwa scientists access to the ship Tangaroa , which enabled regular sea floor mapping of the Ross Sea in Antarctica . <p> He said the " silos " - a relic from the merger - were initially a massive challenge but were ultimately ironed out and he anticipated the same outcome in the new Earth Science PRO , which he described as a " superb coming together " . <p> While he could not comment on the prospect of jobs being lost in the process , he emphasised researchers had been through it all before . <p> " Thirty-three years ago when Niwa was formed money was amazingly tight . We lost our research ship , science was impacted , but over time ... we 've managed to keep the business @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ amazing stuff - and I imagine that 's what 's going to happen in the future . " <p> He believed the recent tough financial years in the wake of Covid were " just a short blip " and said the merger would be a springboard for huge research opportunities . <p> " There 's going to be science undertaken in areas that we 're not even thinking about at the moment . " <p> According to fellow Niwa regional manager and fisheries scientist Phil Jellyman , such research was likely to benefit from technological leaps - a change he had already seen in the technique for tracking salmon , with today 's digital tag and receiver , a far cry from his scientist father 's method ; chasing fish up-river in a jet boat . <p> Computer power was fundamental to much of today 's science , with the current accuracy and realism of climate , flood , and sea-level rise models , inconceivable 10-20 years ago , he said . <p> Although quick to point out it was not his area of expertise , Jellyman said the public @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ forecasting - through the combination of climate research , short-term forecasting knowledge , and access to the same computational power . <p> " Those sorts of advancements only continue to grow , and the forecasts just get better and better and more realistic . Undoubtedly , they 'll be making breakthroughs and producing more accurate and better products for New Zealand . <p> " The number of people that are reliant on good daily forecasting information - for so many of the decisions that are made around the country ... the level of importance it has on the economy , it 's really impressive to think about . " <p> Jellyman said while there was uncertainty about how the organisations would unite , there were clear positives in bringing science together , and many of the researchers had gone through similar upheavals . <p> " We 've got people that were here before Niwa , MetService , GNS , that have been working with us before the 90s that are still with us now , and they 'll still be with us in the new organisation . <p> " You @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ longevity of career - and being able to draw upon that as we move into these new organisations is going to be fantastic . " <p> Piper said that none of the CRIs pre-merger were in a financial position to invest in salaries and capital , and while he would always look to reduce costs , job cuts were not a primary focus . <p> Meanwhile , Bloomfield said he did not anticipate another restructuring following the loss of about 40 roles last year at ESR . <p> He said despite not merging , the overhaul of the science system was significant , and the transition into the institute of Public Health and Forensic Science was more than a rebranding . <p> In addition to the usual " service science " - infectious disease surveillance and forensic testing for police - the shake-up would promote greater collaboration , in particular with universities on cross-disciplinary research projects , Bloomfield said . <h> ' An exciting moment for science ' - Reti <p> Science Minister Shane Reti said " the biggest reset in the last 30 years " was an exciting moment for science , and claimed the size of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ venture capitalists . <p> While the commercial merger happened legally today , uniting the organisations operationally would take longer , he said . <p> Progress was being made on the establishment of the fourth PRO which he expected to be fully-fledged in the next year and which already comprised superconductor specialists the Robinson Research Institute , Reti said . <p> He said its ultimate purpose and composition would be a primary focus of the newly formed Science and Technology Advisory Council when it met in a few weeks ' time - but said it would likely include quantum computing , artificial intelligence , and synthetic biologies hubs . <p> In response to questions about funding the merger by repurposing money for research in Budget 2025 , Reti defended the move , maintaining the system needed to be established before money was poured into it . <p> " The first step needed to be ... get the structure right . We would n't want to put new money substantively into a structure that clearly was n't working . " <p> The new set-up would " more accurately identify priorities and monitor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ science funding , he said . <p> " We know we compare poorly to other OECD countries ... <p> " We should have ambitions towards improving the amount of funding that we have , now that we 've shown we can do a good job with what we 've got . " <p> While the New Zealand Association of Scientists acknowledged that the former Crown Research Institutes had not delivered the economic benefit promised , it was sceptical of the increased focus on commercialisation of science and " deeply concerned that these new organisations , faced with ever-tightening funding constraints , will not deliver systemic change " . <p> Co-president professor Troy Baisden said the best-case scenario would see the PROs make the case for more government support and stable funding . <p> " Without that foundation , we ca n't compete with other nations to attract investment and wo n't generate spin-off and spill-over benefits . @@129515663 <h> Venkat Raman <h> Roshni Thapliyal glows with the silent power of 60 <p> The love and respect go beyond Diamond and Platinum : Professor Anil Thapliyal with his wife Roshni at her 60th Birthday Party held at the Northern Club in Auckland on Friday , September 19 , 2025 ( Photo by Narendra Bedekar , Creative Eye ) <p> Venkat Raman Auckland , September 21 , 2025 <p> Sixty men and women assembled at the Northern Club in Auckland on Friday , September 19 , 2025 , to celebrate the Diamond Jubilee Year of Roshni Thapliyal , wife of Professor Anil Thapliyal , Executive Director of eMental Health International Collaborative ( eMHIC ) . <p> Professor Thapliyal , a globe-trotter advising governments of almost every country in North America , Canada , Europe , the Middle East and Asia Pacific , was firmly on the ground , bringing together old friends to celebrate a truly momentous occasion . <p> The guests joined him in ensuring that Roshni marked her 60th Milestone as a significant day in her life . They accounted for friends and family from Australia , Singapore @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Former Governor-General Sir Anand Satyanand , who was not present , sent a note , " to underline best wishes for a successful , happy event , regarding Roshni 's Birthday this evening . " <p> Glasses clinked , greetings exchanged , laughter echoed , food savoured with delight , and finally , smiles before the camera ! Some of the guests at Roshni Thapliyal 's 60th Birthday Party held at the Northern Club in Auckland on Friday , September 19 , 2025 ( Photo by Narendra Bedekar , Creative Eye ) <p> Profundity among the quiet <p> Professor Thapliyal said that in a world that often celebrates loud voices and visible achievements , it is sometimes the quietest individuals who leave a profound mark . <p> " Roshni exemplifies this truth . Do moments define us , or do people define moments ? Roshni 's life and actions suggest it is the latter , " he said . <p> This event and the words that were shared there were a testament to the deep respect and admiration she has garnered over the years . Known for her infectious @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , Roshni embodies an inspiring blend of humility and passion , consistently choosing kindness and hope as her guiding principles . <p> Laughter brightens the mood and lightens the heart . Roshni being admired by her husband , Professor Anil Thapliyal , at her 60th Birthday Party held at the Northern Club in Auckland on Friday , September 19 , 2025 ( Photo by Narendra Bedekar , Creative Eye ) <p> About Roshni Thapliyal <p> Born and raised in Ba , Fiji , Roshni has built her life in Auckland , where she is known for her gentle strength and unwavering commitment to the people around her . As a wife and partner to Professor Thapliyal , her personal and professional lives are deeply rooted in service and compassion . <p> Professor Thapliyal said that despite her reserved nature , Roshni has quietly become a pillar of hope and support for many . <p> " Her life 's work reflects her resilient spirit . Whether supporting young people or aiding low-resource communities in Fiji , India or New Zealand , Roshni 's dedication is boundless . In recent years , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that are most impacted by climate change , " he said . <p> A sibling ( Reena Heale , right ) and a lifelong friend ( Kamni Singh , left ) made Roshni 's 60th Birthday even more memorable at the Northern Club in Auckland on Friday , September 19 , 2025 ( Photo by Narendra Bedekar , Creative Eye ) <p> Relief to the unfortunate <p> Reflecting that compassion , Roshni pledged on her 60th Birthday to support a school in Koronubu village , Ba , Fiji , and assist those impacted by the devastating monsoon floods in Uttarakhand ( a State in North India , crossing the Himalayas where Professor was born , raised and educated ) . <p> " In the first week of December 2025 , she will travel to Uttarakhand to make her contribution , " Professor Thapliyal said . <p> " Roshni 's story reminds us that moments are fleeting , but people whose actions embody kindness , hope , and resilience are the true architects of change . Her life reiterates that it is not the grandeur of moments but the impact of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the fabric of our communities . As the world continues to evolve , the quiet leaders like Roshni Thapliyal teach us that true strength often whispers , yet speaks volumes , moments that define a lifetime , " he said . <p> Across cultures and generations , the 60th birthday marks a profound milestone , a moment not merely of personal celebration but of collective reverence . In many Asian traditions , including Indian and Chinese , turning 60 signifies the completion of five full cycles of the twelve-year lunar calendar , symbolising a return to one 's birth year and the beginning of a new spiritual phase . It is often seen as a time of renewal , wisdom , and legacy . <p> Until the last Millennium , the 60th year in the Western context was associated with retirement , reflection , and the transition from active duty to mentorship and repose . For many , it is a juncture where life 's achievements are honoured , and the individual is celebrated not just for what they have done but for who they have become -- a leader @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Anil Thapliyal , as the Master of Ceremonies , was at once humorous , emotional and a true host at the 60th Birthday of his wife , Roshni , which he organised at the Northern Club in Auckland on Friday , September 19 , 2025 ( Photo by Narendra Bedekar , Creative Eye ) <p> The Diamond Jubilee <p> The term ' Diamond Jubilee ' has regal origins , historically used to commemorate the 60th anniversary of a Monarch 's reign . Queen Victoria 's Diamond Jubilee in 1897 and that of Queen Elizabeth II in 2012 are among the most notable . <p> The Diamond , chosen for its unmatched durability and brilliance , symbolises the strength , clarity , and enduring value of a life well-lived . When applied to Birthdays , the phrase elevates the occasion from a personal milestone to a communal celebration of resilience , grace , and sparkle . It suggests that the individual , like the gem , has weathered time with elegance and now shines with a light uniquely their own . <p> To provide the best experiences , we use technologies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to these technologies will allow us to process data such as browsing behavior or unique IDs on this site . @@129547698 <h> Turning squashed bugs into climate change data <p> ? What if the insects you hit with your car could contribute to research on climate change ? <p> That 's the idea driving new research from University of Auckland scientists Dr Richard O'Rorke and Dr Aimee van der Reis . <p> They are leading a large team of researchers from the University of Auckland 's School of Computational Science , Centre of Machine Learning for Social Good , School of Biological Sciences , and Nga Ara Whetu -- Centre for Climate , Biodiversity and Society . <p> Over the next year , the scientists and collaborators across the country will ask thousands of members of the public to clean insect material off their licence plates . <p> This will provide insect DNA samples that can be used to map insect populations in different environments across New Zealand . <p> The samples will provide data for AI applications that can identify patterns , predict future insect distribution and track how climate change impacts on these tiny , but ecologically significant , creatures . <p> The United States-based Climate Change AI Innovation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ van der Reis to carry out the research . <p> " The idea for this project comes from something called ' the windscreen phenomenon ' , which reflects how people might notice that more bugs crashed into their windscreen 20 years ago than they do now , " said Dr O'Rorke . <p> Insect studies have often relied on trapping that provides excellent data for a small area , but there is a lack of knowledge about where many thousands of insect species occur across vast areas of New Zealand . <p> " We lack a rough consensus on the number of insect species . To try to fill some of the knowledge gaps , we need new methods to sample insect life over broad swathes of landscape . <p> " This project will help record the insect biodiversity we have now , which will be important to create a baseline for measuring the impacts of climate change and other effects from human activities , " said Dr van der Reis . <p> The researchers have optimized methods to inexpensively identify insects from traces of DNA that remain after they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ information about traces of bacteria , fungi , and plants associated with the insects found on license plates . <p> A pilot project last year was a roaring success , with members of the Auckland Veteran &amp; Vintage Car Club collecting insect DNA from their licence plates . <p> Now , more volunteers from schools , clubs and the wider community will be provided with DNA-sampling kits . The researchers will then extract and sequence DNA from the swabs . <p> To ensure wide coverage , the team is approaching businesses , research groups and local governments in major cities to provide samples from license plates from company cars and public transport vehicles . <p> " We want to engage people who might not necessarily be concerned with climate change , or thinking about insect diversity , and foster a sense of shared responsibility for the ecosystems we 're studying , " Dr O'Rorke said . <p> Insects are vital for everything from pollination of crops that humans depend on to providing a food source for native birds , fishes , lizards and bats . <p> However , climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Changes to weather patterns affect insect habitats and disrupt their life cycles , with more frequent storms , new diseases and invasive species all taking a toll . <p> " Insects are going to be one of the most heavily influenced animals from climate change , " Dr van der Reis said . <p> " We 're going to experience major redistributions of where insects are found and when they develop - and that will have big knock-on effects . <p> " If insects are wiped out in some areas , entire food webs are likely to be disrupted . " 
@@129547734 <p> New Zealand was one of more than 190 countries also part of the Paris agreement within the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change to keep warming within 1.5C above pre-industrial levels . <p> Countries like the United Kingdom , France , Japan and South Korea counted emissions from international shipping and aviation in their targets to some degree . However large emitting nations like China , India and Russia did not . <h> Climate Change Commission wants all emissions accounted in targets <p> The Climate Change Commission carried out its first emissions target review in November , recommending that the transport-related emissions should be included in the targets ( PDF ) . <p> It said in the report there had been significant change in recent years , including greater severity and scale of global warming impacts than what the global science community understood when the target was set . <p> " Many comparable countries have by now set targets that are more ambitious than the current target of Aotearoa New Zealand , " it read . <p> " The increased risks and impacts of climate change have implications @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ costs and risks to future generations . " <p> The Commission also warned about the urgency of the mission to prevent further global warming . <p> " Scientific evidence is increasingly clear that global action is insufficient to limit global warming to 1.5C outright , " it said in its report from November . <p> " This implies that even greater reductions are needed in the near and longer terms to limit as much as possible the amount by which the world exceeds 1.5C , and then to potentially bring the temperature back down again . " <p> It said if no action was taken to reduce emissions from these sectors , they would likely grow to be more than one third of the country 's domestic net emissions by 2050 . <p> New Zealand relied on international shipping and aviation for tourism , business , imports and exports and passenger travel . <p> With carbon dioxide the bulk of the emissions from these industries , the Commission suggested a shift to alternative fuels like sustainable aviation fuel for aviation , and to biofuels or green hydrogen for shipping to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , shipping emissions <p> Climate Change Minister Simon Watts was yet to make a decision , expected to decide by November . <p> An amendment to the Climate Change Response Act would be required to bring these emissions into the targets . <p> In a statement , Watts said New Zealand was not alone in excluding these emissions in domestic targets , and they were still monitored . <p> " Globally , most countries have not included emissions from international aviation and shipping in their domestic targets , " he said . <p> " International shipping and aviation emissions are addressed through globally negotiated mechanisms that New Zealand is a member of . <p> " The International Maritime Organization ( IMO ) addresses international shipping emissions , and the International Civil Aviation Organization ( ICAO ) addresses international aviation emissions . " <p> The bid garnered some industry pushback during public consultation , while further questions remained over the uncertain future of the Emissions Trading Scheme ( ETS ) . <p> ExportNZ said in its submission it did not support the international aviation and shipping emissions being included in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exporters . <p> It wanted national action plans - aligned with goals of the ICAO and the IMO - to be developed before including these emissions in the 2050 goal . <p> The Fertiliser Association said in its submission it believed their inclusion should be delayed , while IMO developed policies on transitioning the shipping sector . <p> It added that New Zealand acting alone instead of collectively would have limited influence on reducing international shipping emissions . <p> Activists from the Ocean Rebellion group protest outside the International Maritime Organization at the end of the 80th Marine Protection Committee conference in London on 7 July , 2023 . @@129563770 <p> If you look for Aotearoa New Zealand on a globe of the world , you 'll see a small scatter of islands in the southern latitudes of the earth 's largest ocean . Our country is far from the heartlands of global politics , and easily forgotten . <p> As a small , remote nation , what are New Zealand 's key competitive advantages for making a living , and thriving ? <p> They include a ' clean , green ' reputation for looking after our spectacular waterways and landscapes ; a sense of integrity that underpins a stable , honest democracy ; mutual goodwill and friendship in dealing with Treaty issues and cultural diversity ; abundant sustainable energy , and an optimistic , positive attitude in tackling difficult challenges . <h> read more <p> As Sir Ernest Rutherford said about his early experiments with physics in New Zealand , ' We did n't have the money , so we had to think . ' That spirit of adventurous , principled inquiry is still alive and well , especially among younger New Zealanders . <p> At present , however @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a sense that key competitive advantages are being squandered , and fundamental values violated . Too many of the qualities and attributes that make New Zealand special in the world are being assaulted . <p> The leaders of our majority parties seem to be flailing around , borrowing old , shopworn ideas that have not worked elsewhere , or new , virulent doctrines that yield chaos , hatred and violence in their countries of origin . <p> In the Beehive at present , Lord Rutherford 's spirit of principled inquiry seems to have vacated the premises . Instead , we see a blitzkrieg of ill thought out legislation , introduced by minor parties that won little public support . <p> Some , including the Treaty Principles Bill , the Regulatory Standards Bill and amendments to the Overseas Investment Act , undermine mutual goodwill , democratic conventions , and the rights and freedoms of New Zealanders to think for themselves and thrive in their own country . <p> Often , the initiatives seem alien , imported from far-right thinkers who are dismantling democracy in the United States , the United Kingdom @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ around the planet . <p> There are those who want to turn New Zealand into a petri-dish for libertarian theories of the Ayn Rand kind , injecting a new brand of nastiness into social life and putting our competitive advantages at risk . <p> While the National Party in New Zealand has traditionally upheld conservative values , the coalition government is an unstable mix , with ideas of austerity , authoritarian inclinations and a strand of populist , divisive politics that is destabilising our society , and the economy . <p> In response , many New Zealanders are leaving the country , while others are in a state of despair as their lives are turned upside down by the collapse of their businesses , the loss of jobs , a rising cost of living and a sense that the Government no longer cares about them . <p> That is not the case everywhere , though . In Tairawhiti , my home region , for example , there are glimmers of light on the horizon . Te Tai ra whiti means ' the coast crossed by the sun ' , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ day -- a great place for flashes of insight , and world-leading ideas . <p> Several weeks ago , a contingent from Tairawhiti helped to launch Think like a Forestat the Beehive , a documentary fronted by Sam Gibson , ' Sam the Trapman ' , and sponsored by Pure Advantage , a group of business people dedicated to advancing New Zealand 's ' clean green ' philosophy in world trade and international relations . <p> Think like a Forest explores the idea of recloaking the most erodible landscapes , and around waterways in New Zealand with indigenous forest , keeping the productive land productive while cleansing waterways , providing habitat for indigenous species , sequestering carbon and generating a long-term income stream for landowners . <p> The idea first emerged in Tairawhiti in 2018 , after an intense storm fuelled by climate change hit Tolaga Bay . This region has some of the most erodible land in the world , and in the storm logs and sediment from industrial plantations inland swept down local rivers , destroying crops , farm buildings , equipment , fences and roads , burying @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the Tolaga Bay disaster , in 2021 the ' Recloaking the Whenua ' conference in Gisborne was held to investigate how to restore indigenous forest to the most erodible land and around waterways . Since then , the idea has flourished in close engagement with Pure Advantage , who built it into a nationwide initiative , ' Recloaking Papatuanuku . ' <p> In 2022 Pure Advantage held a national conference at Te Papa , O Tatou Ngahere / Our Forests , that attracted more than 1000 people . A comprehensive business plan was devised and tested , and an intensive work programme carried out on a ' gold standard ' nature-based carbon credit for New Zealand . <p> In 2023 , however , Cyclone Gabrielle struck Tairawhiti , and this time the damage was widespread , and even more severe . Huge rafts of logs , fallen trees and slash swept down the slopes of highly erodible hillsides , carving out gullies and transporting vast quantities of silt down almost every river across the region . <p> At tight bends in the rivers , the logs and slash cascaded @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , fences and culverts , paddocks , crops , farm buildings , vehicles , equipment and family homes . All the way down river , the rafts of logs collided with river banks , collapsing the banks and depositing more trees and sediment in the river . <p> In Gisborne city , logs and slash piled around the town bridges , creating dams that caused flooding which destroyed and damaged houses and other infrastructure , including communications and the town water supply , and covered the beaches . <p> In the wake of this disaster , Mana Taiao Tairawhiti , an iwi-led group , swung into action , initiating a petition calling for government controls on industrial forestry that attracted 10,000 signatures . A Ministerial Inquiry report , From Outrage to Optimism , recommended a major shift in land use in the region , with the most erodible landscapes restored to indigenous forest . <p> The Think like a Forest documentary features some of those who are leading the way in advancing these ideas . In September 2024 , the Gisborne District Council established a Transition Advisory Group ( TAG @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , farmers and community members all represented . <p> Last week the TAG signed off a draft business plan to present to the Government that upholds the recommendations of the Ministerial Inquiry . An official representing the Government on the TAG called it an ' apex moment , ' and that 's how it felt . <p> More than a hundred years ago , in 1920 , the Director of Forests , Macintosh Ellis , advocated the sustainable management of indigenous forests as the best way of tackling widespread erosion in Tairawhiti . Instead , governments introduced Land Clearance grants and Marginal Lands loans , leading to further erosion . <p> From the late 1950s , ' conservation forests ' of pine trees were planted to try and stabilise the land , but these were privatised in the 1980s and harvested , leading to more devastation across local landscapes . <p> More pine trees were planted in the 1990s , but in Cyclone Gabrielle many of these trees simply fell over , taking the topsoil with them , and were swept down the rivers . <p> When the Transition Advisory @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ land in the region was optimal , it was an epic moment . After more than a century of repeated disasters , geological and ecological realities in Tairawhiti had finally been recognised . <p> Temperate rain forests of the kind that flourish in Tairawhiti perform many functions . With their multi-tiered canopies and ground cover , they buffer heavy rainfall , while their complex root structures help to hold the land together . <p> These forests create a healthy soil sponge rich in bacteria and fungi that holds water during extreme rain and drought , moderating the water cycle , and work together with soil bacteria and fungi to enrich the soil . The water vapour and bacteria they release into the atmosphere help to create clouds and cool the surrounding area . <p> They cleanse waterways , and cool streams , while providing habitat for a rich array of other life forms ; and their diversity makes them more resilient than monocultures to the challenges of climate change . <p> At the same time , international research suggests that with their long-lived trees , temperate rain forests are among @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with perhaps 60% or 70% of the carbon stored underground . <p> For all these reasons , Tairawhiti 's business plan is not just about erosion control , although that is vital . It also has the potential to assist with flood and drought resilience and water and soil quality ; and establish ' gold standard ' permanent carbon sinks , assisting New Zealand to meet its international commitments for climate change and sustainability while upholding its ' clean green ' reputation . <p> If the Government reacts as central government did a century ago , and rejects local knowledge and expert advice , the damage will be global as well as local , and it may be irreversible . <p> In many parts of Tairawhiti , for instance , the land is already back to bedrock , and nothing will grow there for millennia . Fallen trees and rafts of pine logs will keep cascading down the rivers and covering the beaches , devastating the local economy and over time , making the region uninhabitable . <p> Although some politicians argue that in tough times , we ca n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mistaken . That 's precisely why governments were created . <p> The ' green shoots ' that I see in New Zealand are many of our young people . Like those in Think like a Forest -- Sam Gibson , Renee Raroa , Samantha Rowland , Malcolm Rutherford and Graeme Atkins , for instance -- they 're inventive , optimistic and full of ideas . <p> If the major political parties want to adapt and survive , they need to reach out to these younger leaders , and allow them to flourish . As Lord Rutherford said , if you do n't have the money , you have to think , and in this case , thinking like a forest might be timely . <p> The lessons are apposite . Store resources when they are plentiful , and share them in times of scarcity . Be resilient and deeply rooted , and provide shelter from the storms . Embrace diversity , because when tough times come , that enhances your chances of survival . Above all , stay interconnected , and adapt to the world as it changes . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for new life . Mauri tu , mauri ora , mauri noho , mauri mate . Stand up , and live . Sit down , and die . A good warning for leaders , as well as citizens in a democracy . <p> Anne Salmond is a Distinguished Professor at the University of Auckland , and was the 2013 New Zealander of the Year . She became a Dame in 1995 under National , and was awarded the Order of New Zealand in ... Resistance to this current government and being vocal is vital .

@@129586683 <p> In the late 1970s , workers joined Ngati Whatua Orakei to stop luxury housing at Takaparawhau -- a landmark alliance of unions , Maori and environmentalists . <p> During the strike waves of the 1960s and 1970s , " black bans " -- in the unfortunate language of the time -- were a frequent form of work stoppage . They involved unions banning certain forms of work for various reasons . For example , in New Zealand during the 1970s , unions banned trade with Chile ( to protest the Pinochet dictatorship ) and France ( to protest French nuclear testing in the South Pacific ) . <p> Green bans were an innovative progression from black bans . They were ecological political stoppages pioneered in 1970 by Australian construction workers ' unions . The Builders ' Labourers ' Federation placed green bans on disputed land , natural habitats , buildings , and working-class neighbourhoods threatened by developers ( see Rocking the Foundations ) . They only did so after a genuine request from a community group . After a green ban had been placed , construction workers would then @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bans were both a form of workers ' control and ecological control . Their remarkable success in halting expensive construction projects led to repression . However , many of the bans are still observed -- for example they saved several historic Sydney neighbourhoods . <p> Many have championed green bans as examples of how unions can undertake successful direct action to support environmental concerns in this age of climate change , and to subvert the view that the labour movement fundamentally clashes with the environmental one . While the Australian green bans have gained some global recognition , the ones in New Zealand are almost unknown internationally . In Aotearoa , greens bans were also trailblazing -- they were indigenous adaptations of the Australian practice . They were placed to support Maori concerns over their alienated land and fishing grounds in the late 1970s . <p> The most significant green ban in New Zealand was at Takaparawhau/Bastion Point Auckland in 1977 -- 78 . <p> Takaparawhau was the site of a watershed Maori protest against the alienation of their land , and the green ban placed to support the land @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support of Maori in New Zealand history . It was also a globally important example of practical labour union support for indigenous land rights . <p> In the 1840s , Ngati Whatua had gifted much of its Auckland land to the British governor . They retained the Orakei land block ( which included Takaparawhau , so they could still live on their traditional lands ) . Yet , despite an 1869 court ruling that the 700-acre Orakei block was inalienable , the settler government either purchased or compulsorily took almost all of that land by 1951 . <p> In the early 1950s , the government evicted Ngati Whatua Orakei from the tiny amount of land they had left , and burnt their communal meeting house and private houses to a cinder . Joe Hawke , a leader of the Orakei Maori Committee Action Group ( OMCAG ) which organised the occupation of the land in 1977 , was evicted as a youngster along with his family after his home was torched . <p> A belligerent National government , led by prime minister Robert Muldoon , had decided to develop Takaparawhau @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the state above a headland -- into a luxury private housing area . The land was prime real estate with an ocean view . It was also beside a working-class state housing area and " Boot Hill " , where many Ngati Whatua Orakei had been relocated by the state after their early 1950s eviction . The Ngati Whatua iwi and supporters then occupied or repossessed their land , and unions placed a green ban in support . <p> Joe Hawke at Ihumatao in 2019 ( Photo : RNZ / Dan Cook ) <p> In 1977 , Hawke wrote in the Socialist Action newspaper of the 700 acre Orakei block : <p> " Only a quarter acre was left now -- the urupa or burial ground ... Today at Bastion Point , the Ngati Whatua are fighting against the government sub-dividing what is rightfully their land ... Our people will no longer submit to disgrace and humiliation . Bastion Point represents the struggle of the Ngati Whatua for the return of their mana , honour , and ancestral land . <p> The green ban was placed before the occupation began @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ requested that the Auckland Trades Council ( ATC ) ban any work on the site . The ATC was the Auckland region coordinating body of private-sector unions affiliated to the New Zealand Federation of Labour . The acting ATC president Dave Clarke ( of the Te Paatu tribe and the Seamen 's Union ) agreed to it , and the green ban was later confirmed by the full ATC executive . <p> OMCAG representatives then hurriedly contacted the workplaces that would have been required to begin construction work on the land to give practical effect to the green ban . Union job meetings were arranged with such workers , including road metal suppliers and truck drivers , and they voted unanimously to support the ban and to contribute money to the occupation . The unions they contacted contained many Maori , including the labourers and drivers ' unions . Consequently , no development began at Takaparawhau such as bulldozing and infrastructure work like roading . <p> On January 5 , 1977 the occupation began . A large tent town was established to occupy the land . Thousands of supporters visited @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ marae built . Some of the building materials were sourced by unionists and many participated in , or supported , the occupation . <p> Zac Wallace talks to police on eviction day , Takaparawhau 1978 . Wallace was then involved in Aotearoa 's longest workplace conflict at the Mangere Bridge construction site . He later became a union organiser and an actor . ( Photo : Robin Morrison Estate , Auckland Museum , Tamaki Paenga Hira . ) <p> Four months into the occupation , the government threatened to evict the occupiers . They considered the protesters squatters and the tents a shanty town . Despite defying trespass law , the government " had to back off because of the widespread public support for our stand , " according to OMCAG . <p> The committee continued to send its representatives to union meetings to gain support and reinforce the green ban . After meetings , teachers , wharfies/dockers , seafarers , railway workers , construction workers , nurses , meatworkers and others donated money . Brewery workers gifted a weekly levy from their paychecks to the occupiers . However , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that some union officials sold the green ban to unionists as an action opposing the efforts of prime minister Muldoon " to build rich people 's houses on there " to circumvent some union members " who were a bit iffy on Maori rights " . <p> In April 1978 , an injunction was granted to stop protesters trespassing on , using or occupying land at Takaparawhau . OMCAG , in a " special appeal to workers " , called for workers to defend Bastion Point and " show class solidarity with us in our struggle " . The ATC called for a mass union picket if an eviction attempt was made . According to Syd Jackson -- an ATC executive member and major leader in the Maori sovereignty movement -- workers at several job sites went on strike to rush to the occupation when eviction threats were issued . <p> Despite these efforts , the state forcibly evicted the occupiers and arrested 222 people on May 25 , 1978 . Hundreds of supporters , including unionists , could not reach Takaparawhau as the police had sealed off all roads @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Government workers , who were in a conservative public sector union outside the ATC , scabbed on the green ban by demolishing the buildings . The early 1950s eviction had been repeated , despite mass non-violent resistance . <p> To OMCAG , the government had taken off its " mask of democracy " and showed its " true face of state force violence in using 600 police , Army , Navy and Air-Force personnel ... Bastion Point was to be subdivided for a rich elite . The spirit of Maori and Pakeha people in the face of massive state force was tremendous " . Many unionists were among those arrested . The 222 arrests made it one of the largest mass arrests of protesters in the country 's history . <p> Unions locally and globally have generally neglected indigenous issues . The Takaparawhau green ban was an example of a successful practical alliance between indigenous people and unions , as well as environmental action . Its apparent defeat due to state repression turned into a win when , after a Waitangi Tribunal hearing in 1987 , the government eventually @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ had remained undeveloped , and the green ban remained in force after the eviction . Today , much of that land is a public reserve , Takaparawhau Reserve , " for the benefit of all " and managed jointly by Ngati Whatua o Orakei and Auckland Council . <h> More Reading <p> The occupation is now celebrated as a landmark event . It was a turning point in the Maori renaissance . By the 1970s , Maori had lost around 95% of their land since colonisation through war , confiscations and purchases . Takaparawhau was one of the first parcels of land to be returned to Maori through the state 's attempt to redress grievances , which began in the mid-1980s through the Waitangi Tribunal process . The occupation opened the eyes of many Pakeha to the systemic and ongoing nature of land alienation and racism . <p> The green ban was also a high point in workers ' direct action to support Maori land rights . Maori were overwhelmingly concentrated in the blue-collar working-class , and frequently played a central role in many militant unions and strikes . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ were developed during the 1970s -- the largest period of strike and protest activity in the country 's history -- laid the foundations for the green ban . Several other green bans were placed on traditional fishing grounds and alienated lands in the 1970s . But after unions were progressively defeated and then hollowed out in the 1980s and 1990s by de-industrialisation and neoliberalism , green bans were not placed again as far as is known . <h> Subscribe <h> Events <h> FOLLOW US <h> DOWNLOAD THE APP <p> The Spinoff is subject to NZ Media Council procedures .
